Chapter 4: Meeting Michael — The Man Who Saved My
Recovery / The Sweet Science of Transformation
I was pissed off, frustrated, and losing hope fast. The therapists before Michael were nice
— too nice. They'd praise every tiny twitch like it was a gold medal, accept "goodenough,
" and avoid pushing hard so I wouldn't get upset or frustrated. Progress slowed to
a crawl. I started believing I'd hit my wall — that this was it.
Then came Michael.
He was new when I rolled in — fresh off the stroke, totally limp on my left side, no AFO
yet. He was 3–4 inches taller than me but at least 100 lbs lighter. I looked up at this lanky
dude and thought,
"Good luck with this sack of oranges, buddy.
"
But he didn't hesitate. He put my dead arm over his shoulder, propped my limp leg and
twisted Drop Foot ankle against his foot, and said,
"Start walking.
" I laughed and scoffed
under my breath — what did I have to lose? It was his back, not mine.
So we walked. Ten feet at a time. That's all I could handle without collapsing. He never let
me fall. Always looked out for my best interest — safe pushes, but real pushes. No
reckless crap, just calculated challenges that made me grow.
By the end of my two months at Encompass Health (first of eight inpatient stays), we were
making full laps around the third-floor therapy gym. We laughed hysterically the whole
way. Bro jokes flying nonstop: "Bro,
" "Brocephus,
" "Bro-ntasaurus Rex,
" "Brodie"
— you
name it. Felt like one of the boys again. Such a great guy.
He even came over one night after my discharge. Brought a case of beer. Score one for
the stroke survivor! We practiced walking a bit, then sat down, cracked open some cold
ones, and watched football like normal dudes. Hung out, laughed, talked shit. Felt human
again. Major milestone. Almost every other friend I had disappeared after the stroke —
vanished like smoke. All except my cousin Kasey — more like a brother. Our moms are
sisters; we grew up as brothers — sports, scooters, weights, even got addicted to opiates
together in the early 2000s opioid mess. He visited me in the hospital. Seeing my brother
from another mother was relief after all the family fights over visitation.
Michael gave me that back — camaraderie, laughs, belief when I had none. He was the
unlikely savior who turned "I can't" into "watch this.
" And yeah, the beer didn't hurt.
The Tough Love That Wasn't Mean — It Was Real Care
Michael's philosophy was simple and brutal in the best way:
●
●
●
●
No participation trophies for showing up.
Push beyond comfort zones every session — safely.
Celebrate breakthrough, not just effort.
Treat me like an athlete who forgot how to compete, not a fragile patient.His favorite line: "Comfortable is the enemy of recovered.
"
He was always looking out for my best interest — never reckless, always calculated. Safe
enough to trust, hard enough to grow.
The Breakthrough Session That Proved It
We were working on walking patterns. My left leg dragged, gait all messed up. Every
other therapist accepted it as "pretty good considering.
"
Michael said,
"This isn't walking — this is limping with style. We're fixing this.
"
He broke down the muscle firing, targeted the weak spots, created drills, made me repeat
until perfect. Refused "good enough.
" One session — noticeable improvement that others
said would take months.
High Expectations vs. Low Expectations
Low expectations kill: reduce effort, create self-limiting beliefs, turn plateaus into parking
lots, breed learned helplessness.
High expectations (done safely) build: increased effort, growth mindset, breakthrough
seeking, extraordinary results.
Michael encouraged — he challenged me to exceed my own expectations, refused
artificial limits, created impossible-but-doable exercises, set breakthrough goals.

●
"Pain is temporary, quitting is permanent.
" Discomfort = progress. Push through

●
(safely).

rewires.

" No finish line. Keep improving
indefinitely.
"You're not recovering to your old self — you're creating a new self.
" Stroke is
opportunity for transformation. New you can be better.

Strength accelerated.
Confidence skyrocketed.

Believed in breakthroughs again.

Builder’s Note
The right person believing in you at the right time changes everything. Michael proved
that — with safe pushes, bro jokes, and a case of beer. Now prove it for yourself — and
for the next survivor watching.Chapter 5: The First Build Site — From ICU Survivor to
Rehab Warrior
You survived the initial blast. Now, the real construction begins. Leaving the ICU is like
getting airlifted out of a war zone—there is relief, but a new, completely different fight
awaits. This isn't about staying alive anymore; it’s about re-learning how to live.
My first stop: Encompass Health, the first of eight inpatient rehab stays. That statistic
alone tells you everything you need to know about the long-haul commitment required.
The ICU was about flickers of life; rehab is about the unglamorous, repetitive, day-in,
day-out grind. This is where you trade the life support machine for a hard hat and a tool
belt.
The Shock of Repetition
In the ICU, every tiny movement was a miracle. In rehab, every tiny movement is a
mandatory repetition. The environment changed from an emergency room to a
construction site.
●
●
ICU Focus (Survival Phase): Stabilize, survive, and keep the lights on.
Rehab Focus (Construction Phase): Repetition, practice, and rebuilding the body’s
blueprint from scratch.
It was here I learned the difference between healing (passive, time-based) and
construction (active, effort-based). Healing happens to you. Construction you do yourself.
The first few weeks were a psychological shock: endless transfers, balance drills, and the
soul-crushing weight of knowing this wasn’t a sprint. It was a marathon where every mile
was uphill. The biggest trap in rehab is letting the routine become a rut.
The Law of Forced Growth
The most painful tool in the rehab arsenal was the parallel bars. I stood there, channeling
all my focus into making my left leg step forward. It was a war of nerves. My brain sent
the signal—Move!—but the leg remained dead weight, dragging and scraping the floor.
One session, trying a drill Michael called the "Step-Tap-Hold,
" I stumbled, caught myself
on the bars, and swore out loud. Michael, who always knew the score, just said: "Your
brain is not broken, Nick. It’s rerouting. It needs the map. You have to draw that map with
sweat.
"That’s the Law of Forced Growth: You have to force the brain to fire new pathways
through relentless, specific, and often frustrating repetition. You can't just wait for the
power to come back on; you have to rewire the house. Discomfort is not the enemy;
comfortable is the enemy.
The Hidden Victory: Community
The ICU was isolating. Rehab was the first time I was surrounded by other
warriors—stroke survivors and traumatic brain injury patients who understood the
"chainsaw in the skull" feeling. We shared the dark humor, the tiny wins (like feeding
yourself without spilling), and the absolute certainty that the person next to you was
fighting the same invisible war.
This community became the first external component of the Sasquatch Armor. Hope is
contagious. You see a survivor walking, and a voice in your head screams,
"If they can do
it, watch me.
" Rehab wasn't just therapy; it was the boot camp where I learned to use
frustration as fuel. It’s where I started transforming from a patient into the architect of my
own future.
Builder’s Blueprint: Early Rehab Focus
1.
2.
3.
Ditch the "Patient" Mindset: You're not sick; you're in training. This is a job. Show
up and punch the clock.

Chapter 6: Your Brain's Incredible Healing Power / Your
Recovery Machine — The Brain That Never Quits
Your brain isn't broken — it's a machine that's been damaged but still has the power to
rebuild itself. The stroke hit hard, carved a path of destruction through my left
hemisphere, left me paralyzed on one side, struggling to speak, swallow, think straight.
Doctors said "extensive damage,
" "permanent deficits,
" "plateau after year one.
" They
were looking at averages. I was looking at possibility.
The Brain That Refuses to Quit
Neuroplasticity is the brain's ability to reorganize itself by forming new neural connections
throughout life. After stroke, undamaged areas can take over lost functions. New
pathways form. Old ones strengthen. It's not magic — it's mechanics. Your brain is
designed to adapt, survive, thrive.
When I first tried to move my left hand, nothing. Zero. But every attempt, every flicker,
every failed rep sent a signal. The brain listened. It started building bridges around the
damage. Slow. Painful. Invisible for months. But it never stopped.
Builder’s Science: How Rewiring Actually Works
●
●
●
BDNF (Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor): Exercise, repetition, novelty release
this "fertilizer" for neurons. More BDNF = more growth, stronger connections.
Hebb's Law: "Neurons that fire together wire together.
" Every time you attempt a
movement (even if it fails), neurons fire. Repeat enough, they wire new pathways.
Use It or Lose It: Idle areas weaken. Forced use (therapy, daily reps) strengthens
them.●
●
Bilateral Training: Strong side teaches weak side. Mirror therapy tricks the brain
into seeing movement on the paralyzed side.
Intensity Matters: Low-effort therapy gives low results. High-rep, high-focus work
rewires faster.
I didn't wait for the brain to heal itself. I attacked it with reps, tools, experiments. The brain
responded — not because it had to, but because I gave it no choice.
My Proof: From 0% to 90%+
●
●
●
●
Year 1: Flickers, tiny movements, first assisted steps.
Year 2–3: Walking without AFO, basic arm use.
Year 4–5: Strength gains, bilateral coordination, speech clarity.
Year 6–7: Memory doubled again, athletic movements, still rising.
Doctors said plateau after year one. I said "watch me.
" The brain kept rewiring because I
kept feeding it signals.
Universal Truth: Any Brain Can Adapt
This isn't just for stroke. Any setback — addiction, trauma, depression, business failure —
damages neural pathways. Same rules apply: starve the bad habits, feed the new ones
with repetition, intensity, novelty. The brain rewires. Always.
Reflection Point

input. Watch it rebuild.

Neuroplasticity Revolution — Recovery NEVER Stops
The doctors gave you a timeline. They whispered things like "plateau" and "what you get
in the first year is mostly it.
" They meant well. They were wrong. They were looking at the
average patient who stops swinging the hammer. You are not average. You are a warrior
enrolled at Sasquatch Recovery University, and here, we recognize one truth: Your brain
is still on the clock.
Neuroplasticity is the brain’s ability to reorganize itself by forming new neural
connections. It’s not a one-time event that shuts off after 12 months; it’s a living,
breathing, constantly updating construction site. Every time you try to move a weak limb,
every time you practice a difficult word, you send an electrical jolt through the damaged
circuitry. If the signal finds an alternative route, it fires. Do it again. And again. The path
that was a muddy goat track on Day 1 becomes a paved freeway on Day 1,000.
The Builder’s Law of Forced Input
The brain will only rewire what you force it to use. It’s a resource manager. Why build a
new bridge if the old one isn't being used? You need three things to force growth:
1.
2.
3.
Intensity: Low-effort, passive movements (like a few gentle stretches) are a waste
of time. Your brain needs high-rep, high-focus work to trigger the growth factor
(BDNF) that acts as the soil for new neural pathways.
Repetition: The goal is not 10 reps a day; the goal is 100 or 1,000. Do the
movement until the signal is no longer a whisper but a scream. Monotony is the
mother of all breakthroughs.
Novelty: Once a movement becomes easy, change the task. Stand on a different
surface. Use a weight. Close your eyes. The brain learns best when it’s forced to
problem-solve under pressure.
The Proof: I was told my biggest gains would be in year one. My biggest, most
jaw-dropping wins—like walking without the AFO, doubling my memory processing, and
building the Stroke Recovery Academy—happened in years 4, 5, 6, and 7. The recovery
never stopped because I never stopped feeding it input. The timeline is a suggestion for
quitters. For us, recovery is infinite.
Reflection Point

Chapter 8: Finding My Way: From Wheelchair to Walking —
How I Taught Myself to Walk and Almost Totally Cured My
Drop Foot
When I left the ICU, walking was an embarrassing stumble. My left foot, dead weight from
the knee down, was a hazard, not a tool. Drop foot is the enemy of liberation; it locks you
back in the wheelchair or to the safety of a cane. Doctors offered the Ankle-Foot Orthosis
(AFO) as a permanent solution. I saw it as a temporary training wheel. This chapter is the
demolition plan for drop foot.
The Blueprint: Deconstruction and Rebuild
The core of conquering drop foot is simple: the anterior tibialis muscle, which lifts your
foot, is weak, and the brain has forgotten its command signal. You must re-establish the
connection through three specific, forced steps:
1.
2.
3.
Isolated Signal Fire: Start non-weight-bearing. Sit and concentrate on wiggling
your left toes and lifting your whole foot. Try to twitch the muscle. Do this until it
burns. The sheer act of intention is the signal.
Forced Lengthening (Negative Pressure): As covered in Chapter 13, I used the
Negative Pressure Therapy hack. Stand (assisted!) and slowly lean forward,
putting maximum downward pressure on the front of the ankle and foot. This
forces the muscle to activate eccentrically (while lengthening). It hurts like hell. It
works like a miracle.
The Step-Tap-Hold Drill: Michael's genius. In the parallel bars, shift your weight
onto your strong side. Now, with your weak leg, lift the foot just enough to clear theground, tap the heel, and hold the foot up for a full second before placing it
forward. The hold is the game-changer. It trains the anterior tibialis to fire under
load.
The Proof: For years, I did these drills hundreds of times a day. I taped weights to my foot
for resistance. I walked on grass, then pavement, then hills. I was obsessed with that
'hold' command. Today, I walk without an AFO. I’ve gone from shuffling to a confident,
athletic stride. The nerve damage didn't disappear, but the brain found a new, superior
highway.
Reflection Point
If your focus is on the big goal of "running again,
" you'll fail. Focus on the tiny goal of a
"perfect toe clearance.
" Pick one drill from above and commit to 50 repetitions every day
this week. The details of the step build the path to the stride.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Drop foot isn't permanent nerve damage—it's temporary lack of input. You are the one
who delivers the command. Never stop fighting for your stride. A warrior doesn't shuffle;
a warrior walks with purpose.
"
Builder’s Note
Don't wait for your brain to give you a natural gait. You have to build the gait, rep by rep,
until your brain accepts it as the new default setting.
Chapter 9: The 66-Day Transformation — How Consistency
Rewires Everything
You can have the greatest blueprint in the world (this book), the fiercest resolve, and the
best physical therapist (your Michael), but if you are not consistent, you will fail. The
66-Day Transformation is the non-negotiable construction schedule. It’s based on a
simple truth: it takes an average of 66 days for a new behavior to become automatic. Not
21 days, not 30. Sixty-six.
The Blueprint: From Intent to InevitableThe 66-Day Transformation is a non-stop, 24/7 commitment to a single, measurable
recovery habit.
1.
2.
3.
Identify ONE Keystone Habit: Don't choose five things. Choose one. Example: "I
will do 100 reps of isolated hand-opening per day,
" or "I will stand unaided for a
total of 15 minutes a day.
"
The Non-Negotiable Rule: You must complete the habit for 66 straight days. If you
miss one day, you go back to Day 1. There is no negotiating with the schedule. This
is how you prove to your brain that this new habit is more important than the old
pattern (learned non-use).
The Anchor: Anchor the habit to an existing routine. Example: "I will do my 100
reps immediately after my morning coffee,
" or "I will do my standing practice while
watching the 6 o'clock news.
" This removes the need for willpower.
The Proof: The 66-Day commitment is what took me from twitches to functional
movement. I started with standing for one minute. Day 1 was brutal. Day 66, it was
automatic. I ran multiple 66-Day programs back-to-back: one for walking, one for hand
opening, one for speech clarity. Consistency is the compound effect of recovery—small,
daily choices that lead to massive, inevitable breakthroughs.
Reflection Point
What is the one, single, measurable habit that would unlock your next phase of recovery?
Write it down. Get your 66-Day Tracker (Appendix G) and mark today as Day 1. If you miss
tomorrow, you fail. Start over. Don't let your old self win.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"The brain is a stubborn contractor. It only builds the foundation when the work shows up
every single day. Consistency is the bridge between possible and inevitable. Show up for
66 days or start digging a grave for your potential.
"
Builder’s Note
The 66-Day Transformation forces the neural pathways to become permanent. It’s not
about intensity here; it’s about persistence.Chapter 10: The Kremers Recovery Formula / The
Sasquatch Formula —
TAKE→LEARN→EXPERIMENT→COMBINE→APPLY
Recovery isn't a medical textbook—it's a massive, chaotic engineering problem. The
therapists before Michael were stuck in the "prescription" mindset: do this, no more, no
less. Michael and I were in the "experimentation" mindset. You need a system for this, and
this is the formula forged on the floor of the rehab gym.
The Sasquatch Formula (T-L-E-C-A): Your Operating System
1.
TAKE (The Input): Start with whatever you can get. Take the advice from a doctor,
a snippet from a study, a drill from a therapist, or an idea from a fellow survivor.
Don't filter, just take the raw material.
2.
LEARN (The Science): Why did they tell you to do it? Read the study. Ask your
therapist. Understand the muscle, the nerve, the neuroplastic principle at play. You
cannot fix the engine without knowing the mechanics.
3.
EXPERIMENT (The Lab): This is where you become the architect. Take the input
and modify it. Change the angle. Add a weight. Increase the speed. Do it standing.
Do it sitting. My Negative Pressure Therapy (Chapter 13) and Baseball Bat Therapy
(Chapter 12) were born here. The official way failed. So I invented a better way.
4. COMBINE (The Blueprint): Mix the successful experiments. Combine mirror
therapy with a movement drill. Combine weight-bearing with an external stim
device. The combination of successful micro-hacks is what creates the true
breakthrough.
5.
APPLY (The Scale): Once you have a successful combination, you execute the
66-Day Transformation (Chapter 9). This is the scaling phase—the monotonous,
unrelenting application that makes the breakthrough permanent.
The Proof: Doctors told me my drop foot was permanent (TAKE). I read studies on
eccentric loading and forced dorsiflexion (LEARN). I started leaning against a bar, forcing
the weak muscle to fight against gravity and my own weight (EXPERIMENT). I combined
that with the Step-Tap-Hold drill (COMBINE). I did this for 66 days straight, then 66 more
(APPLY). Result? No AFO.
Reflection Point
What one piece of standard, boring therapy are you doing today? Now, apply
EXPERIMENT. How can you change it to make it 10x harder, more specific, or more
focused on your current goal? That uncomfortable change is where the growth is hiding.Sasquatch’s Steel
"You are not a patient; you are a Chief Engineer. Never follow a blueprint blindly. Question
the methods, embrace the chaos of the experiment, and use every failure as data to
invent your next breakthrough.
"
Builder’s Note
The formula is your roadmap to extraordinary results. It turns information into knowledge,
and knowledge into power.
Chapter 11: Think-Twitch-Move: The Core Progression /
Think-Flinch-Existence — From Paralysis to Power
The ultimate humiliation of the stroke is the disconnect between your mind and your body.
The mind screams,
"MOVE!" and the limb replies with absolute, dead silence. This gap is
the battlefield of the Think-Flinch-Existence progression—a deep-brain hack that restores
the primal connection.
The Blueprint: Re-establishing Command
This progression is about brute-forcing the signal to the brainstem, bypassing the
damaged cerebral cortex until new pathways are strong enough.
1.
2.
3.
Think (The Intention): Close your eyes. Place 100% of your focus on the single,
smallest movement you want. Don't just wish for it—send a white-hot, singular
command to that limb. Think-Twitch.
Flinch (The Breakthrough): A flinch is the raw, unplanned, involuntary movement
that precedes a controlled voluntary one. It’s a cough, a sneeze, a shock, or a
sudden, explosive effort that causes a flicker of movement. When you get a flinch,
celebrate it like a firework. This is the brain proving the line is open. Immediately
after, try the Think again.
Existence (The Default): This is the phase where the limb starts to activate under
load, during daily life, without conscious effort. The limb exists in your active
world. You can scratch an itch. You can use it to stabilize. The connection is
permanent; the movement is becoming automatic.
The Proof: My first flicker (Chapter 1) was a small twitch in my left hand. I used that twitch
as the foundation. For weeks, I would Think and try to replicate that twitch. I would Thinkabout moving my left hand while driving, while talking, while eating. Slowly, the twitches
turned into tiny, unreliable movements. Now, my hand fires automatically for balance and
grip. I went from the Think phase (total conscious effort) through the Flinch phase
(involuntary response) to the Existence phase (automatic function).
Reflection Point
Stop trying to move your hand or foot by moving your hand or foot. Sit in total silence and
focus only on the signal. Spend one minute trying to generate a flinch—that white-hot
command. Did you feel anything? A tingle? A warmth? A whisper? That’s the brain
listening. Write it down.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Paralysis is not silence; it is a broken speaker. The mind is still shouting the command.
Your job is to replace the wiring, one tiny pulse at a time. The flinch is proof you are
winning the war for control.
"
Builder’s Note
This is the mental warfare section. The intention is the first movement. The first flicker of
life in the limb is a direct result of relentless, focused intention.
PART III: THE ARSENAL —
BREAKTHROUGH TECHNIQUES THAT
ACTUALLY WORK
Chapter 12: First Steps: Small Victories Build Big
Comebacks / Baseball Bat Therapy — My Secret Weapon
Most therapists avoid spasticity (stiffness/contractures) because it is hard to manage.
They tell you to stretch gently. I call bullsh**
. Spasticity is a coiled spring—it’s bad tone,
but it’s still tone. My goal was to turn that bad tone into functional power. Baseball Bat
Therapy was my first, most successful, and most controversial experiment.The Blueprint: From Spasm to Function
WARNING: This is a high-intensity, un-prescribed, self-administered technique.
CONSULT YOUR PHYSICIAN/THERAPIST BEFORE ATTEMPTING.
The goal is to force the spastic muscles (flexors) to relax by overwhelming the opposing
muscles (extensors) with ballistic, highly resistive movement.
1.
2.
3.
4.
The Tool: I used a standard, heavy wooden baseball bat.
The Setup: With a therapist (Michael) spotting me, I would grip the bat with my
strong right hand. My weak left hand would grip as much as it could (it was mostly
spastic flexion).
The Drill (Controlled Swing): I would execute a slow, highly focused two-handed
swing motion. The left arm had no choice but to follow. The resistance of the bat
forced the flexors to let go so the extensors could try to complete the movement.
The Result: The spasticity in my left arm would temporarily shatter. I’d have a
5-minute window of semi-controlled movement immediately after the session. I
used that window for highly focused hand/arm drills.
The Proof: This was the first technique that unlocked my left arm. The bat was too heavy
and the movement too fast for my spasticity to win. It was a neurological circuit breaker. It
broke the lock. It was painful, exhausting, and borderline reckless, but it gave me my first
moments of controlled, functional movement in my arm. I would not have functional hand
use today without the Baseball Bat.
Reflection Point
What is the biggest "no" you get from your body? If it's a tight fist, the answer isn't gentle
stretching. It's safe, forced, repetitive counter-movement. You need to find your own
metaphorical 'bat'
—a tool or exercise that safely forces the weak side to submit to a
high-intensity command.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Spasticity is a wall of concrete. You don’t ask it nicely to move—you bring a wrecking
ball. The fear of pain is less damaging than the paralysis of learned non-use. Fight fire
with fire, but always have a spotter.
"
Builder’s Note
Find the safe line between intense training and injury. The most significant progress lives
right there, at the edge of your comfort zone. Comfortable is the enemy of recovered.Chapter 13: Weight Bearing & Negative Pressure Therapy /
Weight Bearing Revolution — Standing Your Way to
Strength
Weight bearing isn't just exercise — it's medicine. After the stroke, my left leg was dead
weight. No tone, no control, no hope from most therapists. They said "use the AFO
forever" and "don't put too much pressure on it — you might hurt yourself.
" I heard "stay
weak.
" I chose to stand anyway.
Why Weight Bearing Works
Standing loads the bones, muscles, joints, and nervous system. It sends signals upward:
"Hey brain — this leg still exists. Wake it up.
" Gravity + body weight + intention =
neuroplasticity on steroids.
●
●
●
●
●
Bones get stronger (Wolff's Law — bone remodels under stress).
Muscles fire against gravity (anti-gravity extensors activate).
Proprioception rebuilds (brain relearns where the leg is in space).
Circulation improves (no more pooling blood in a limp limb).
Brain gets massive input — every second standing is thousands of neural signals.
I started with parallel bars and Michael's shoulder. Ten seconds felt like ten hours. But
every second counted. The brain was listening.
Negative Pressure Therapy — My Secret Hack
This was my own twist — using controlled "negative" (downward) pressure to force
activation.
How I did it:
●
●
●
●
●
Stood against a wall or bar.
Leaned forward slowly so body weight pressed into ankles/feet.
Held 30–60 seconds — felt the stretch in calves, anterior tibialis fighting to lift toes.
Repeated 10–20 times per session.
Progressed: less wall support, longer holds, added small weights.
Why it worked:●
●
●
●
Eccentric loading strengthens muscles during lengthening (most stroke therapies
focus only on concentric).
Stretches spastic muscles safely.
Forces dorsiflexion under load — directly attacks drop foot.
Builds confidence — every hold proved "this leg can support me.
"
Combined with weight bearing, this became my revolution. From zero to standing
independently. From dragging to walking. From AFO to freedom.
Safety First — But Don't Baby Yourself
Listen to your body. Start assisted. Build slowly. But don't let fear stop progress. Pain =
injury? Stop. Discomfort = growth? Keep going (safely).
Universal Application
Weight bearing isn't just for stroke legs.
●
Business feeling shaky? Put weight on it — take real risks, lean in, feel the
●
●
pressure.
Relationship falling apart? Stand in the discomfort — face the hard talks.
Health slipping? Put weight on better habits — stand against gravity every day.
Reflection Point
Where in your life are you avoiding weight? Write it down. Set one small "stand" this week
(literal or figurative). Feel the pressure. Let it build strength. Your heart's still thumpin'
—
so your life is still pumpin'
. Get knocked down five times, stand up six.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Stand even when standing hurts. Weight bearing isn't punishment — it's medicine. Lean
into the pressure. The Sasquatch didn't stay seated. Neither should you.
"
Builder’s Note
Weight bearing turns dead weight into living strength. Don't wait for permission. Stand.
Feel gravity. Rewire your brain. Keep swinging.Chapter 14: Mirror Therapy Magic — Tricking Your Brain to
Heal
Mirror therapy was one of the most powerful, stupidly simple tools I used. A mirror. A box.
Some duct tape. And a brain that can be fooled into rewiring itself.
How It Works
●
●
●
●
Place mirror vertically between arms/legs.
Good side in front of mirror, paralyzed side hidden behind.
Move good side — watch reflection as if paralyzed side is moving.
Brain sees "movement" on bad side → mirror neurons fire → damaged pathways
get stimulated.
I did this 10–20 minutes daily. Watched my right hand open/close, imagined left doing
same. Felt weird at first — like lying to my brain. But it wasn't a lie. It was training.
My Results
●
●
●
●
Early weeks: Nothing visible, but I felt tingles, warmth in left hand.
Month 2–3: Small twitches when mirroring.
Month 4+: Left hand started opening on its own during sessions.
Long term: Bilateral coordination improved dramatically.
Science backs it: Mirror therapy activates mirror neuron system, increases excitability in
damaged motor cortex, reduces learned non-use. It's cheap, safe, and stupidly effective.
Universal Application
Mirror therapy isn't just for paralyzed limbs.
●
●
●
Confidence shattered? Mirror your strong self — act "as if" until it becomes real.
Skill rusty? Watch someone great (or your past self on video) and mimic.
Fear blocking you? Visualize success in the mirror — trick your brain into believing
it's possible.
Reflection Point
What part of your life feels "paralyzed"? Set up a mirror (literal or mental). Watch your
strong side succeed. Imagine the weak side following. Do it daily for 10 minutes. Your
brain can be tricked into healing. Trick it.Sasquatch’s Steel
"Mirror therapy fools the brain — but the results are real. Watch the strong side win. Make
the weak side jealous. Then make it join the party. The Sasquatch didn't wait for
movement — I created it. So can you.
"
Builder’s Note
A mirror, some imagination, and relentless reps — that's all it takes to start rewiring. Don't
wait for the brain to catch up. Trick it into catching up. Keep swinging.
Chapter 15: Conquering Drop Foot and Reclaiming Your

Understanding Drop Foot
Peroneal nerve damage → weak anterior tibialis → foot drops → drag toes → trip → fear →
less walking → weaker muscles → worse drop foot. Vicious cycle.
I broke it with a multi-attack strategy.
My Drop Foot Destruction Arsenal
1.
2.
3.
4.
Ankle Dorsiflexion Drills (100+ reps/day)
○
Seated: Lift toes toward shin with band resistance.
○
Standing: Heel walks (walk on heels only).
Negative Pressure / Eccentric Loading
○
Stand on step edge. Lower heels below step slowly (eccentric). Rise onto
toes (concentric).
○
Wall leans: Lean forward, press ankles into dorsiflexion under body weight.
Mirror Therapy + Mental Rehearsal
○
Watch good foot lift in mirror. Imagine left doing same. 10–20 min/day.
AFO Ditching Protocol (Risky — Do Safely)
○
Started short walks without AFO (held wall/Michael).○
Built confidence in ankle stability.
○
Gradual outdoor use. Never fell hard — adjusted when needed.
5. 66-Day Consistency Streak
○
Locked in daily reps for 66 days. Habit formed. Brain rewired. Foot lift
became automatic.
Results
●
●
●
Year 1–2: AFO required, dragging bad.
Year 3: Short walks without AFO possible.
Year 4+: Drop foot almost gone. Walk with little limp. Natural stride reclaimed.
Universal Lesson
Every "drop foot" in life — bad habit, fear, weakness — can be destroyed the same way:

"permanent.
"

Mirror success mentally.

Chapter 17: The Motivation Engine That Never Quits /
NEVER SURRENDER — The Mindset That Changes
Everything
Motivation isn’t some fluffy feeling that shows up when the stars align. It’s a machine you

rage — but rage burns out. What kept me going through years of flickers that felt like
nothing, plateaus that felt permanent, and nights where quitting looked easier than
breathing? A system. The Motivation Engine.
This chapter is that engine laid bare. No fairy tales. No "just believe" nonsense. Just the
brutal, battle-tested mechanics that turned despair into drive, and drive into 90%+
recovery — and still rising.
Why Most People Lose the Motivation War
You’ve seen it. Hell, I lived it. Someone starts strong — gym, diet, business, recovery —

quit.
The reason isn’t lack of willpower. It’s lack of system. Motivation is not a gift — it’s an
engineering problem. Build the engine right, and it runs even when everything else is on
fire.

Chapter 21: Home Again: Adapting to Your New Reality /
Home Sweet Home — Conquering Daily Life
Coming home from the hospital was supposed to be a victory lap. It felt like walking into a
war zone with no weapons. No nurses on call. No therapy schedule. Just me, a
wheelchair, dead weight left side, and a house that suddenly felt like it was built for
giants. Stairs became mountains. Doorways became choke points. Shower became a
potential drowning hazard. Kitchen counters became unreachable. I was a 250-lb
Arkansas boy turned prisoner in my own home — fully wheelchair-bound or crawling on
all fours like some kind of broken animal.
But home wasn't the end of recovery. It was where the real fight started. Here's how I
turned that house into my personal training ground and conquered daily life one brutal,
humiliating, victorious rep at a time.
The Home Reality Shock
●
Mobility Hell: Slide board transfers to bed or toilet — one slip and I was on the
●
●
●
floor again.
Bathroom Wars: Raised toilet seat (thank God), grab bars in both showers (my
Person
father installed them), shower chair at first. Sponge baths from .
Catheter crystallized after two months — removal felt like fire. Blood everywhere. I
got front-row seats to the fight and passed out 10 seconds in. Good thing I love
looking at my own blood so much or I might have really passed out! /sarcasm
Kitchen Nightmare: Counters too high. Couldn't reach anything. Pureed food
phase lingered. Appetite came roaring back — gained 80 pounds chillin' and
wheelin'
. Beard hid the gobbler. Belt sighed in relief twice a day: once putting pants
on, once taking them off.
Isolation Peak: Friends vanished like smoke. Family fights over visitation
restrictions. Depression crushed me — laid in bed 24/7, smoked like a chimney,
fantasized about steering into traffic to end it. Felt like a burden ruining everyone's
life.
My father stepped up big: installed a wheelchair ramp on the front porch so I could get in
and out without being carried. Put handrails in both showers. Raised toilet seat. Shower
chair. He didn't say much — just built. That quiet action spoke louder than any pep talk.The Turning Point — Fatherhood Fire
My son was born while I was still wheelchair-bound. Couldn't even stand for the delivery.
Watched from the chair as his little pale body poked up over Person
's horizon. That
moment hit like a freight train: I was a father. But what kind of father could I be stuck
seated? How could I play catch, teach him to throw a spiral, show him how to be a man —
from a chair?
I made a promise right there: "I'll give everything to be the best father possible. I'll play
catch with both arms.
" That why pulled me out of bed when nothing else could. Purpose >
pity. Fatherhood became my ultimate motivation — the fire that turned home from prison
to proving ground.
Home Adaptation Arsenal
1.
2.
3.
4.

Ramp & Transfer Mastery Father's ramp = freedom to leave house. Slide board
practice daily — from embarrassing falls to smooth transfers.

standing (with handrail). No more sponge baths. Independence won.
Kitchen Takeover Reacher tool at first. One-handed cooking drills. Grip reps built
strength. Ate for recovery — high protein, BDNF boosters (eggs, nuts, fish). Weight
gain? Fuel for muscle rebuild.
Daily Life Reps Brushing teeth, buttoning shirts, opening doors — forced functional
tasks. Every struggle = rep. TikTok filming = accountability + tribe.

humiliation a story. Every small win proof: "I'm still thumpin'
. Life's still pumpin'
"

Chapter 24: Returning to Work and Purpose / 90% and
CLIMBING — Why I'm Still Fighting
My original identity was tied to being a provider, an athlete, a man of strength. After the
stroke, I was none of those things. The question wasn't if I'd return to work, but what my
work would be. The answer: my recovery became my job, and my purpose became my
revenue.
The Identity Crisis
Being dependent, losing my career, and being stuck at home felt like a failure. But I
realized that the fight itself—the refusal to quit—was the most valuable asset I had. I took
the blueprint of my comeback and started selling it.
The Pivot: From Patient to CEO
1.
Document the Struggle: My raw, unedited videos on TikTok showed the struggle
and the wins. This built trust.
2.
Productize the Blueprint: The "Sasquatch Formula,
" the "66-Day Tracker"
(Appendix G), and the mindset became digital products and courses. This became
Stroke Recovery Academy (part of StrokeLyfe.org).
3. Scale the Impact: I couldn't coach everyone, but I could teach thousands through
online courses. Impact scaled, and so did revenue.
4.
The New Work: My work is fighting my own recovery and teaching others to fight
theirs. It's rewarding, purpose-driven, and profitable. This is the Empire Building
phase.90% and Still Climbing
I am at 90%+ function, but the fight is not over. Why?
●
●
●

Accountability: My tribe watches. I can't quit on them.

My greatest setback became my greatest purpose and my new, more valuable career.

Your review is the fuel that helps this blueprint reach the next warrior who is told "never.
"

About the Author
Nicholas "Stroked-Out Sasquatch" Kremers is a stroke survivor, entrepreneur, and
relentless advocate for limitless recovery. After suffering a massive hemorrhagic stroke in
2018 that left him paralyzed and wheelchair-bound, he was told he would never walk
normally again. He defied every expectation, achieving over 90% function, and now
dedicates his life to teaching other survivors the non-traditional,
"battle-tested" methods
that led to his impossible comeback. He is the founder of Stroke Recovery Academy and
the StrokeLyfe.org movement, based in Fort Smith, Arkansas.Tab 4Tab 5Tab 6THE ULTIMATE STROKE

>PART I: THE STORM — THE
BREAKDOWN TO
BREAKTHROUGH
Chapter 1: The Day Everything
Changes / Decem
ber 3, 2018 —
The Day I Refused to Die
One moment I was built like a brick wall — benching over four hundred pounds,
working long days, feeling unbreakable. Then, the next, half my body went silent. The
lights didn’t go out completely that day; they just flickered. My left side stopped
listening. My arm hung heavy, my leg dead weight, words crumbling on my tongue.
I tried to tell myself it was exhaustion, dehydration — anything but what it was. But
the truth hit like a sledgehammer: I’d had a massive hemorrhagic stroke.
The Body That Betrayed Me
I’d always been the strong one — the athlete, the protector, the worker. Football,
basketball, baseball, gymnastics as a kid. If it took grit, I was in.
So when my body quit, it wasn’t just medical. It was an identity collapse. How does a
man built on strength function when strength is gone? Lying in that hospital bed, Iwasn’t thinking about recovery or inspiration. I was thinking about survival — and the
terrifying thought that my own body had become a prison.
The Chainsaw in My Skull
The pain wasn’t what broke me — it was the confusion. It felt like chainsaws buzzed
behind my eyes. I even reached up once, sure I’d find steel under my scalp. Every
beep of a monitor mocked me. I couldn’t fix this with muscle or willpower. The same
body that once obeyed every command now ignored me completely.
And then came the humiliation. In the ICU, I lost 50 pounds — down to tube
feedings, then pureed foods that tasted like cardboard mush. Doctors said the
stroke hit areas that would wipe out music recognition — I’d never know a Britney
Spears song from a chainsaw again, couldn’t even tell a hot dog from a burger by
sight or sound. Boy were they wrong!
One day, while dealing with the straight-up embarrassment of pooping myself
(incontinence hitting hard from the brain damage),
"Oops... I Did It Again
" blasted in
my head. Not just blasting — I sang it out loud, as loud as I could, which was a damn
feat considering I had just graduated from tubes to purees. Nurses probably
thought I was losing it, but it was proof: music was still there, appetite intact, brain
still firing in ways they said it wouldn’t.
When I got home, it flipped — gained 80 pounds chillin
'
and wheelin
' in my
wheelchair. I grew a beard to hide the gobbler that developed. My belt sighs in relief
twice a day: once when I put my pants on, and again when they come off at night!
The weight scale wishes the docs had locked that one down as fact.
That’s when I realized: recovery wasn’t going to be about strength. It was going to be
about communication — and hope.
Builder’s Science: The First Signal
After a stroke, the brain’s communication network misfires — neurons lose
connection to the body’s
"blueprints.
" But under the right conditions, neuroplasticitykicks in: new pathways form, rerouting messages through surviving tissue. It’s like
rewiring a circuit board after an explosion — slow, delicate, but possible.
Every attempt to move, even a failed one, sends sparks through that rewiring
process. Every flicker counts.
(Reference: Kleim & Jones, 2008; NIH Neurorehab Review, 2021)
The Moment Everything Changed
When I met Person
Michael, my physical therapist, he didn’t look like much — tall,
lanky, unassuming. But he carried belief like a toolbelt.
He didn’t baby me. He didn’t promise miracles. He just said,
survive, Nick. We’re here to rebuild.
"
"We’re not here to
That was the first flicker — not in my muscles, but in my mindset. The idea that
maybe, somehow, I could build again.
From Athlete to Patient
My new sport was standing. My new gym, a hallway lined with parallel bars. Each
step was a victory; each failure was data.
Person
Michael would say,
"Don’t think of it as exercise — think of it as practice.
"
Practice meant repetition. Repetition meant growth.
He had me chase balloons to retrain balance, climb stairs with assistance, and use
tools no textbook mentioned. Every creative challenge sent a new signal to my brain.
It wasn’t about muscle — it was about rewiring.
That’s the thing about recovery: you don’t rebuild by routine. You rebuild by
problem-solving under pressure — one flicker at a time.
The First FlickerThe day my left hand twitched, it didn’t look like much. But to me, it was a miracle. A
spark of life inside a body I’d already grieved. I stared at that twitch like a mechanic
watching a dead engine fire once, just once, after months of silence.
That was the first time I understood what progress really was. Not leaps. Flickers.
Not miracles. Messages.
That tiny movement proved that the lights might dim — but they never truly go out.
Reflection Point
What was your
fighting back?
"flicker
"
moment — the first tiny sign that your body or mind was still
How did it feel? Write it down. That flicker is proof your heart is still thumpin
'
your life is still pumpin
'
. Celebrate it today — no matter how small.
— so
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Chop wood when you
'
re tired. Stand even when standing hurts. Celebrate flickers
as if they were fireworks. Hope is contagious — without it, I'd have started digging
my own grave years ago. Everyone needs hope and a purpose. Find yours today.
"
Builder’s Note
The lights may flicker, but they never go out if you keep swinging.<br clear=
"
all"
>Chapter 2: ICU Hell — Where I
Learned to Fight Back / The Two
Paths: Limitation or Liberation
The ICU wasn
't just a room — it was hell with beeps and fluorescent lights. It was a
month-long stay that nearly broke me. Morphine turned my brain into a funhouse
mirror — hallucinations where nurses were plotting against me, people who weren
't
there, feeling trapped in some medical experiment. Medical explanation: brain
swelling and meds. Real explanation: your own mind turning against you when you
need it most.
The Communication & Swallowing Nightmare
Words wouldn
't come. My thoughts clear in my head, but my mouth refused to
cooperate. Swallowing? Gone. I started on tube feedings — that damn tube down
my throat. One night I woke up choking on it. Person
Lisa freaked out, yelling for
help. Response was slow. She didn
't wait — ripped that tube out like a boss. Blood,
chaos, nurses rushing in. We might not have a romantic relationship anymore, but
'
we
re still a team raising our son. That moment? Pure warrior mode from her.
After tubes came pureed food — tasted like baby mush, took an hour to swallow
crushed meds in applesauce. Doctors said the stroke location meant I'd lose music
recognition, and couldn
't tell a hot dog from a burger. Wrong again. Britney blasted
through the pain, and the appetite survived.
The Dependency & Humiliation Hits
Everything taken for granted became impossible: rolling over, reaching water,
adjusting the blanket, scratching an itch, bathroom dignity. The psychological hit
was brutal — suddenly dependent on others for basic human stuff. This destroys
self-worth. But it also teaches you to fight for independence like hell.One day, coming back from rehab, I had to use a slide board to transfer from the
wheelchair to the bed. Mobility was trash. I slipped and fell hard on the tile floor.
Couldn
't move. Three people tried lifting me — no chance. They finally called in the
Hoyer lift — hoisting me up like a 20k-pound I-beam on a skyscraper. Degrading.
Embarrassing. Nothing I could do.
Then the catheter story. Two months with that thing in — supposed to be changed
and cleaned regularly. Nope. Crystallized to my member. When discharge came, the
nurse removed it. Hurt like the dickens. Blood dripped down my leg, on the floor. I
got front-row seats to the fight — knocked out cold 10 seconds in.
Lisa
'
s Loyalty During the Chaos
Person
Lisa was pregnant through all this. She rarely left my side — only to
shower. Weeks after the stroke, she found out it was a boy. Our baby boy. She
stayed. Held my pee cup, gave sponge baths, fought the system. Thank you,
Person
Lisa — for our son, for being there when everything was falling apart.
The Darkest Thoughts
Brutal honesty: I wondered if everyone would be better off if I didn
't make it. Burden.
No independence. The best days behind me. Depression after stroke is real — it can
kill recovery before it starts. Those thoughts are normal. Dangerous. You
'
ve gotta
fight them with purpose.
The Hardest, Most Unbearable, Slow, Yet Uber Rewarding Experience
This stroke has been the hardest, most unbearable, slow grind of my life. Yet it'
s also
the most rewarding. Forced me to rebuild everything — body, mind, purpose. Taught
me hope is contagious. Purpose is non-negotiable. Without it, I'd have dug my own
grave years ago.
The Two Paths: Limitation or LiberationThe ICU is where you face the ultimate choice: Limitation or Liberation.
●
●
Limitation is accepting the doctor
'
s statistics, feeling guilt, and letting
dependency destroy your fight.
Liberation is using the humiliation and the darkest thoughts as fuel, fighting
for every small sign of movement (every flicker), and choosing to rebuild for a
purpose bigger than your pain.
The gap between impossible and inevitable is your refusal to quit. Your hell is your
launching pad.
Reflection Point
What was your most humiliating hospital moment? Write it down. Then write:
"That
moment doesn
't define me. It'
s fuel.
" Share it with your tribe if you can — it helps
others feel less alone. Hope spreads when we own the hard parts.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"ICU hell shows what you
'
re made of. Curveballs filled with lead and chainsaw blades
build character. Oh well — gut check time. Dig deep, find purpose, spread hope.
Without it, grave-digging starts. With it? Empire building. The Sasquatch has your
back — we
'
re watching. You
'
re valuable. Keep swinging.

Chapter 3
: The Compound Effect of
Choosing Possible
Rock bottom ain
't the end — it'
s the cleanest slate you
'll ever get. When everything
'
s
stripped away, there
'
s no clutter, no excuses, no
"
what ifs
" left to hide behind. I hit it
hard in that ICU bed — 50 staples in my skull, left side paralyzed, mind spinning from
hallucinations, body betraying me at every turn. Doctors whispering
"
"
permanent,
family fighting over visitation, me wondering if the people I loved would be better off
without the burden. That'
s rock bottom. But here
'
s the truth nobody tells you: rockbottom is the most solid foundation you can build on. Nothing left to lose means
everything left to gain.
The Choice That Defines Everything
Every morning in recovery, you wake up and make a choice that decides your whole
future. Most people don
't even realize they
'
re making it — it happens on autopilot.
But once you see it clearly, you take control.
The choice is simple:
Probable or Possible?
Probable is what statistics and doctors sell you:
●
●
●
●
●
Most recover 60–70% at best.
Big gains stop after 6–12 months.
Significant improvement unlikely after 2 years.
Adjust expectations to avoid disappointment.
Be
"
realistic
"
— manage limitations, accept the new normal.
Possible is what happens when you refuse the sales pitch:
●
●
●
●
What if I push beyond the timeline?
What if I experiment until something clicks?
What if I treat "
"
never
as a dare?
What if my brain rewires farther than anyone predicts?
Probable feels safe — predictable, comfortable, backed by experts. But it costs you
everything: your true potential, breakthrough opportunities, the chance to become
proof that the rules were incomplete.
I made my choice in that hospital bed. A doctor looked at my scans and said,
"You
'll
probably never walk normally again. We need to focus on helping you adapt.
" In that
instant, I could have nodded, accepted it, learned to live small.Instead, I treated it as one person
'
s best guess based on limited data. I chose
possible.
"Watch me.
"
The Rebellion Against "Never
"
When someone says
"
"
never,
what they
'
re really saying is:
●
●
●
●
"I don
't know how.
"
"It'
s statistically unlikely.
"
"I haven
't seen it before.
"
"It would take extraordinary effort.
"
They
'
re not saying:
●
●
●
●
"It'
s impossible.
"
"Your brain can
't change.
"
"You should stop trying.
"
"Miracles don
't happen.
"
My answer to every
"
never
"? Watch me prove it wrong.
The Science of Belief
Research backs this up hard:
●
●
●
Patients who believe recovery is possible recover more function (positive
expectations increase therapy compliance).
Hope and optimism improve neuroplasticity (belief drives BDNF release,
strengthens new pathways).
Belief in continued improvement leads to continued improvement
(self-fulfilling prophecy in the right direction).
Your mind isn
't along for the ride — it'
s driving the bus. Choose possible every day,
and the bus goes farther.
My Daily Choice RitualEvery morning I did the same thing:
Instead of "What can
't I do today?" → "What might be possible today?"
Instead of focusing on losses → Focused on potential.
Instead of "This is as good as it gets
" → "This is where the real work begins.
"
Those small daily choices compounded. Day 1: Try moving a finger instead of
accepting paralysis. Week 1: Attempt standing instead of accepting wheelchair life.
Month 1: Work on walking instead of immobility. Year 3: Pursue athletic activities
instead of basic function. Year 7: Still rising, still proving.
The Three Levels of Possibility Thinking
Level 1: Basic Possibility
"Maybe I can recover some function.
"
Better than nothing, but still capped by conventional expectations.
Level 2: Expanded Possibility
"Maybe I can recover more than doctors expect.
"
Open to pushing timelines, unconventional methods. Progress accelerates.
Level 3: Unlimited Possibility
"Maybe I can prove the impossible is possible.
"
No artificial limits. Constant experimentation. Recovery becomes a platform for
helping others.
I started at Level 1 in that hospital bed. Evolved to Level 3. You can too.The Compound Effect of Choosing Possible
Small choices build massive breakthroughs:
●
Day 1: Choose to try moving a finger → Week 1: Attempt standing → Month 1:
Work on walking → Year 3 (after 2.5 years in a wheelchair): Pursue athletic
activities instead of basic function → Year 7: Still climbing, building an
academy.
Momentum becomes unstoppable.
The Possibility Mindset in Action
Instead of "I'll never be able to
" → "I haven
___
't figured out how to
"
___ yet.
Instead of "Doctors say impossible
" → "Doctors haven
't seen someone like me
before.
"
Instead of "Be realistic about limitations

Chapter 5: The First Build Site —
From ICU Survivor to Rehab
Warrior
You survived the initial blast. Now, the real construction begins. Leaving the ICU is
like getting airlifted out of a war zone—there is relief, but a new, completely different
fight awaits. This isn
't about staying alive anymore; it’s about re-learning how to live.
My first stop: Encompass Health, the first of eight inpatient rehab stays. That
statistic alone tells you everything you need to know about the long-haul
commitment required. The ICU was about flickers of life; rehab is about the
unglamorous, repetitive, day-in, day-out grind. This is where you trade the life
support machine for a hard hat and a tool belt.
The Shock of RepetitionIn the ICU, every tiny movement was a miracle. In rehab, every tiny movement is a
mandatory repetition. The environment changed from an emergency room to a
construction site.
●
●
ICU Focus (Survival Phase): Stabilize, survive, and keep the lights on.
Rehab Focus (Construction Phase): Repetition, practice, and rebuilding the
body’s blueprint from scratch.
It was here I learned the difference between healing (passive, time-based) and
construction (active, effort-based). Healing happens to you. Construction you do
yourself. The first few weeks were a psychological shock: endless transfers, balance
drills, and the soul-crushing weight of knowing this wasn’t a sprint. It was a
marathon where every mile was uphill. The biggest trap in rehab is letting the routine
become a rut.
The Law of Forced Growth
The most painful tool in the rehab arsenal was the parallel bars. I stood there,
channeling all my focus into making my left leg step forward. It was a war of nerves.
My brain sent the signal—Move!—but the leg remained dead weight, dragging and
scraping the floor.
One session, trying a drill Person
Michael called the
"Step-Tap-Hold,
" I stumbled,

You have to draw that map with sweat.

That’s the Law of Forced Growth: You have to force the brain to fire new pathways
through relentless, specific, and often frustrating repetition. You can
't just wait for
the power to come back on; you have to rewire the house. Discomfort is not the
enemy; comfortable is the enemy.

The Brain That Refuses to QuitNeuroplasticity is the brain
'

connections throughout life. After stroke, undamaged areas can take over lost
functions. New pathways form. Old ones strengthen. It'

Your brain is designed to adapt, survive, thrive.
When I first tried to move my left hand, nothing. Zero. But every attempt, every

Builder’s Science: How Rewiring Actually Works
●
●
●
●
●

release this

" for neurons. More BDNF = more growth, stronger

Hebb'

"Neurons that fire together wire together.
" Every time you

new pathways.
Use It or Lose It: Idle areas weaken. Forced use (therapy, daily reps)
strengthens them.
Bilateral Training: Strong side teaches weak side. Mirror therapy tricks the
brain into seeing movement on the paralyzed side.
Intensity Matters: Low-effort therapy gives low results. High-rep, high-focus
work rewires faster.
I didn
't wait for the brain to heal itself. I attacked it with reps, tools, experiments. The
brain responded — not because it had to, but because I gave it no choice.

>Chapter 7: Neuroplasticity: The

"

"

Neuroplasticity is the brain’s ability to reorganize itself by forming new neural
connections. It’s not a one-time event that shuts off after 12 months; it’s a living,
breathing, constantly updating construction site. Every time you try to move a weak
limb, every time you practice a difficult word, you send an electrical jolt through the
damaged circuitry. If the signal finds an alternative route, it fires. Do it again. And
again. The path that was a muddy goat track on Day 1 becomes a paved freeway on
Day 1,000.
The Builder’s Law of Forced Input
The brain will only rewire what you force it to use. It’s a resource manager. Why build
a new bridge if the old one isn
't being used? You need three things to force growth:
1.
2.
Intensity: Low-effort, passive movements (like a few gentle stretches) are a
waste of time. Your brain needs high-rep, high-focus work to trigger the
growth factor (BDNF) that acts as the soil for new neural pathways.
Repetition: The goal is not 10 reps a day; the goal is 100 or 1,000. Do the
movement until the signal is no longer a whisper but a scream. Monotony is
the mother of all breakthroughs.3.
Novelty: Once a movement becomes easy, change the task. Stand on a
different surface. Use a weight. Close your eyes. The brain learns best when
it’s forced to problem-solve under pressure.
The Proof: I was told my biggest gains would be in year one. My biggest, most
jaw-dropping wins—like walking without the AFO, doubling my memory processing,
and building the Stroke Recovery Academy—happened in years 4, 5, 6, and 7. The
recovery never stopped because I never stopped feeding it input. The timeline is a
suggestion for quitters. For us, recovery is infinite.

>Chapter 8: Finding My Way: From
Wheelchair to Walking — How I
Taught Myself to Walk and Al
most
Totally Cured My Drop Foot
When I left the ICU, walking was an embarrassing stumble. My left foot, dead weight
from the knee down, was a hazard, not a tool. Drop foot is the enemy of liberation; it
locks you back in the wheelchair or to the safety of a cane. Doctors offered the
Ankle-Foot Orthosis (AFO) as a permanent solution. I saw it as a temporary training
wheel. This chapter is the demolition plan for drop foot.
The Blueprint: Deconstruction and Rebuild
The core of conquering drop foot is simple: the anterior tibialis muscle, which lifts
your foot, is weak, and the brain has forgotten its command signal. You must
re-establish the connection through three specific, forced steps:
1.
2.
3.
Isolated Signal Fire: Start non-weight-bearing. Sit and concentrate on
wiggling your left toes and lifting your whole foot. Try to twitch the muscle. Do
this until it burns. The sheer act of intention is the signal.
Forced Lengthening (Negative Pressure): As covered in Chapter 13, I used
the Negative Pressure Therapy hack. Stand (assisted!) and slowly lean
forward, putting maximum downward pressure on the front of the ankle and
foot. This forces the muscle to activate eccentrically (while lengthening). It
hurts like hell. It works like a miracle.
Person
The Step-Tap-Hold Drill: Michael'
s genius. In the parallel bars, shift
your weight onto your strong side. Now, with your weak leg, lift the foot just
enough to clear the ground, tap the heel, and hold the foot up for a full
second before placing it forward. The hold is the game-changer. It trains the
anterior tibialis to fire under load.The Proof: For years, I did these drills hundreds of times a day. I taped weights to
my foot for resistance. I walked on grass, then pavement, then hills. I was obsessed
with that 'hold'
command. Today, I walk without an AFO. I’ve gone from shuffling to a
confident, athletic stride. The nerve damage didn
't disappear, but the brain found a
new, superior highway.
Reflection Point

running again,
"
you
'll fail. Focus on the tiny goal of a
"
perfect toe clearance.
" Pick one drill from above and commit to 50 repetitions
every day this week. The details of the step build the path to the stride.

"Drop foot isn
't permanent nerve damage—it'

one who delivers the command. Never stop fighting for your stride. A warrior doesn
't
shuffle; a warrior walks with purpose.

Chapter 9: The 66-Day
Transformation — How
Consistency Rewires Everything
You can have the greatest blueprint in the world (this book), the fiercest resolve, and
the best physical therapist ( Person
your Michael), but if you are not consistent,
you will fail. The 66-Day Transformation is the non-negotiable constructionschedule. It’s based on a simple truth: it takes an average of 66 days for a new
behavior to become automatic. Not 21 days, not 30. Sixty-six.
The Blueprint: From Intent to Inevitable
The 66-Day Transformation is a non-stop, 24/7 commitment to a single, measurable
recovery habit.
1.
2.
3.
Identify ONE Keystone Habit: Don
't choose five things. Choose one. Example:
"I will do 100 reps of isolated hand-opening per day,
"
or
"I will stand unaided
for a total of 15 minutes a day.
"
The Non-Negotiable Rule: You must complete the habit for 66 straight days.
If you miss one day, you go back to Day 1. There is no negotiating with the
schedule. This is how you prove to your brain that this new habit is more
important than the old pattern (learned non-use).
The Anchor: Anchor the habit to an existing routine. Example:
"I will do my
100 reps immediately after my morning coffee,
"
or
"I will do my standing
practice while watching the 6 o
'
clock news.
" This removes the need for
willpower.
The Proof: The 66-Day commitment is what took me from twitches to functional
movement. I started with standing for one minute. Day 1 was brutal. Day 66, it was
automatic. I ran multiple 66-Day programs back-to-back: one for walking, one for
hand opening, one for speech clarity. Consistency is the compound effect of
recovery—small, daily choices that lead to massive, inevitable breakthroughs.
Reflection Point

recovery? Write it down. Get your 66-Day Tracker (Appendix G) and mark today as
Day 1. If you miss tomorrow, you fail. Start over. Don
't let your old self win.
Sasquatch’s Steel"The brain is a stubborn contractor. It only builds the foundation when the work
shows up every single day. Consistency is the bridge between possible and
inevitable. Show up for 66 days or start digging a grave for your potential.

Chapter 10: The Kremers Recovery
Formula / The Sasquatch Formula
—
TAKE→LEARN→EXPERIMENT→COMBIN
E→APPLY
Recovery isn
't a medical textbook—it'
s a massive, chaotic engineering problem. The
therapists before Person
Michael were stuck in the
"
prescription
"
mindset: do
this, no more, no less. Person
Michael and I were in the
"
experimentation
"
mindset. You need a system for this, and this is the formula forged on the floor of
the rehab gym.
The Sasquatch Formula (T-L-E-C-A): Your Operating System
1.
TAKE (The Input): Start with whatever you can get. Take the advice from a
doctor, a snippet from a study, a drill from a therapist, or an idea from a fellow
survivor. Don
't filter, just take the raw material.2.
LEARN (The Science): Why did they tell you to do it? Read the study. Ask
your therapist. Understand the muscle, the nerve, the neuroplastic principle
at play. You cannot fix the engine without knowing the mechanics.
3.
EXPERIMENT (The Lab): This is where you become the architect. Take the
input and modify it. Change the angle. Add a weight. Increase the speed. Do it
standing. Do it sitting. My Negative Pressure Therapy (Chapter 13) and
Baseball Bat Therapy (Chapter 12) were born here. The official way failed. So I
invented a better way.
4. COMBINE (The Blueprint): Mix the successful experiments. Combine mirror
therapy with a movement drill. Combine weight-bearing with an external stim
device. The combination of successful micro-hacks is what creates the true
breakthrough.
5.
APPLY (The Scale): Once you have a successful combination, you execute
the 66-Day Transformation (Chapter 9). This is the scaling phase—the
monotonous, unrelenting application that makes the breakthrough
permanent.
The Proof: Doctors told me my drop foot was permanent (TAKE). I read studies on
eccentric loading and forced dorsiflexion (LEARN). I started leaning against a bar,
forcing the weak muscle to fight against gravity and my own weight (EXPERIMENT). I
combined that with the Step-Tap-Hold drill (COMBINE). I did this for 66 days
straight, then 66 more (APPLY). Result? No AFO.
Reflection Point
What one piece of standard, boring therapy are you doing today? Now, apply
EXPERIMENT. How can you change it to make it 10x harder, more specific, or more
focused on your current goal? That uncomfortable change is where the growth is
hiding.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"You are not a patient; you are a Chief Engineer. Never follow a blueprint blindly.
Question the methods, embrace the chaos of the experiment, and use every failure
as data to invent your next breakthrough.

Chapter 11: Think-Twitch-Move:
The Core Progression /
Think-Flinch-Existence — From
Paralysis to Power
The ultimate humiliation of the stroke is the disconnect between your mind and
your body. The mind screams,
"MOVE!" and the limb replies with absolute, dead
silence. This gap is the battlefield of the Think-Flinch-Existence progression—a
deep-brain hack that restores the primal connection.
The Blueprint: Re-establishing Command
This progression is about brute-forcing the signal to the brainstem, bypassing the
damaged cerebral cortex until new pathways are strong enough.
1.
2.
3.
Think (The Intention): Close your eyes. Place 100% of your focus on the
single, smallest movement you want. Don
't just wish for it—send a white-hot,
singular command to that limb. Think-Twitch.
Flinch (The Breakthrough): A flinch is the raw, unplanned, involuntary
movement that precedes a controlled voluntary one. It’s a cough, a sneeze, a
shock, or a sudden, explosive effort that causes a flicker of movement. When
you get a flinch, celebrate it like a firework. This is the brain proving the line is
open. Immediately after, try the Think again.
Existence (The Default): This is the phase where the limb starts to activate
under load, during daily life, without conscious effort. The limb exists in youractive world. You can scratch an itch. You can use it to stabilize. The
connection is permanent; the movement is becoming automatic.
The Proof: My first flicker (Chapter 1) was a small twitch in my left hand. I used that
twitch as the foundation. For weeks, I would Think and try to replicate that twitch. I
would Think about moving my left hand while driving, while talking, while eating.
Slowly, the twitches turned into tiny, unreliable movements. Now, my hand fires
automatically for balance and grip. I went from the Think phase (total conscious
effort) through the Flinch phase (involuntary response) to the Existence phase
(automatic function).
Reflection Point
Stop trying to move your hand or foot by moving your hand or foot. Sit in total
silence and focus only on the signal. Spend one minute trying to generate a
flinch—that white-hot command. Did you feel anything? A tingle? A warmth? A
whisper? That’s the brain listening. Write it down.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Paralysis is not silence; it is a broken speaker. The mind is still shouting the
command. Your job is to replace the wiring, one tiny pulse at a time. The flinch is
proof you are winning the war for control.
"
Builder’s Note
This is the mental warfare section. The intention is the first movement. The first
flicker of life in the limb is a direct result of relentless, focused intention.<br

Chapter 12: First Steps: Small
Victories Build Big Comebacks /
Baseball Bat Therapy — My Secret
Weapon
Most therapists avoid spasticity (stiffness/contractures) because it is hard to
manage. They tell you to stretch gently. I call bulls***
. Spasticity is a coiled
spring—it’s bad tone, but it’s still tone. My goal was to turn that bad tone into
functional power. Baseball Bat Therapy was my first, most successful, and most
controversial experiment.
The Blueprint: From Spasm to FunctionWARNING: This is a high-intensity, un-prescribed, self-administered technique.
CONSULT YOUR PHYSICIAN/THERAPIST BEFORE ATTEMPTING.
The goal is to force the spastic muscles (flexors) to relax by overwhelming the
opposing muscles (extensors) with ballistic, highly resistive movement.
1.
2.
3.
4.
The Tool: I used a standard, heavy wooden baseball bat.
The Setup: With a therapist ( Person
Michael) spotting me, I would grip the
bat with my strong right hand. My weak left hand would grip as much as it
could (it was mostly spastic flexion).
The Drill (Controlled Swing): I would execute a slow, highly focused
two-handed swing motion. The left arm had no choice but to follow. The
resistance of the bat forced the flexors to let go so the extensors could try to
complete the movement.
The Result: The spasticity in my left arm would temporarily shatter. I’d have a
5-minute window of semi-controlled movement immediately after the
session. I used that window for highly focused hand/arm drills.
The Proof: This was the first technique that unlocked my left arm. The bat was too
heavy and the movement too fast for my spasticity to win. It was a neurological
circuit breaker. It broke the lock. It was painful, exhausting, and borderline reckless,
but it gave me my first moments of controlled, functional movement in my arm. I
would not have functional hand use today without the Baseball Bat.
Reflection Point
What is the biggest "
"
no
you get from your body? If it'
s a tight fist, the answer isn
't
gentle stretching. It'
s safe, forced, repetitive counter-movement. You need to find
your own metaphorical 'bat'
—a tool or exercise that safely forces the weak side to
submit to a high-intensity command.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Spasticity is a wall of concrete. You don’t ask it nicely to move—you bring a
wrecking ball. The fear of pain is less damaging than the paralysis of learned
non-use. Fight fire with fire, but always have a spotter.
"Builder’s Note
Find the safe line between intense training and injury. The most significant progress
lives right there, at the edge of your comfort zone. Comfortable is the enemy of
recovered.<br clear=
"
all"

Chapter 13
: Weight Bearing &
Negative Pressure Therapy /
Weight Bearing Revolution —
Standing Your Way to Strength
Weight bearing isn
't just exercise — it'
s medicine. After the stroke, my left leg was
dead weight. No tone, no control, no hope from most therapists. They said "
use the
AFO forever
"
and "don
't put too much pressure on it — you might hurt yourself.
" I
heard "
stay weak.
" I chose to stand anyway.
Why Weight Bearing Works
Standing loads the bones, muscles, joints, and nervous system. It sends signals
upward:
"Hey brain — this leg still exists. Wake it up.
" Gravity + body weight +
intention = neuroplasticity on steroids.
●
●
●
●
●
Bones get stronger (Wolff'
s Law — bone remodels under stress).
Muscles fire against gravity (anti-gravity extensors activate).
Proprioception rebuilds (brain relearns where the leg is in space).
Circulation improves (no more pooling blood in a limp limb).
Brain gets massive input — every second standing is thousands of neural
signals.I started with parallel bars and Person
Michael'
s shoulder. Ten seconds felt like
ten hours. But every second counted. The brain was listening.
Negative Pressure Therapy — My Secret Hack
This was my own twist — using controlled "
negative
" (downward) pressure to force
activation.
How I did it:
●
●
●
●
●
Stood against a wall or bar.
Leaned forward slowly so body weight pressed into ankles/feet.
Held 30–60 seconds — felt the stretch in calves, anterior tibialis fighting to
lift toes.
Repeated 10–20 times per session.
Progressed: less wall support, longer holds, added small weights.
Why it worked:
●
●
●
●
Eccentric loading strengthens muscles during lengthening (most stroke
therapies focus only on concentric).
Stretches spastic muscles safely.
Forces dorsiflexion under load — directly attacks drop foot.
Builds confidence — every hold proved "this leg can support me.
"
Combined with weight bearing, this became my revolution. From zero to standing
independently. From dragging to walking. From AFO to freedom.
Safety First — But Don
't Baby Yourself
Listen to your body. Start assisted. Build slowly. But don
't let fear stop progress. Pain
= injury? Stop. Discomfort = growth? Keep going (safely).
Universal Application
Weight bearing isn
't just for stroke legs.●
●
●
Business feeling shaky? Put weight on it — take real risks, lean in, feel the
pressure.
Relationship falling apart? Stand in the discomfort — face the hard talks.
Health slipping? Put weight on better habits — stand against gravity every
day.
Reflection Point
Where in your life are you avoiding weight? Write it down. Set one small "
stand" this
week (literal or figurative). Feel the pressure. Let it build strength. Your heart'
s still
thumpin
'
— so your life is still pumpin
'
. Get knocked down five times, stand up six.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Stand even when standing hurts. Weight bearing isn
't punishment — it'
s medicine.
Lean into the pressure. The Sasquatch didn
't stay seated. Neither should you.
"
Builder’s Note
Weight bearing turns dead weight into living strength. Don
't wait for permission.
Stand. Feel gravity. Rewire your brain. Keep swinging.<br clear=

Chapter 14:
Mirror Therapy Magic
— Tricking Your Brain to Heal
Mirror therapy was one of the most powerful, stupidly simple tools I used. A mirror. A
box. Some duct tape. And a brain that can be fooled into rewiring itself.
How It Works
●
●
Place mirror vertically between arms/legs.
Good side in front of mirror, paralyzed side hidden behind.●
●
Move good side — watch reflection as if paralyzed side is moving.
Brain sees
"
movement"
on bad side → mirror neurons fire → damaged
pathways get stimulated.
I did this 10–20 minutes daily. Watched my right hand open/close, imagined left
doing same. Felt weird at first — like lying to my brain. But it wasn
't a lie. It was
training.
My Results
●
●
●
●
Early weeks: Nothing visible, but I felt tingles, warmth in left hand.
Month 2–3: Small twitches when mirroring.
Month 4+: Left hand started opening on its own during sessions.
Long term: Bilateral coordination improved dramatically.
Science backs it: Mirror therapy activates mirror neuron system, increases
excitability in damaged motor cortex, reduces learned non-use. It'
s cheap, safe, and
stupidly effective.
Universal Application
Mirror therapy isn
't just for paralyzed limbs.
●
Confidence shattered? Mirror your strong self — act "
as if"
until it becomes
●
●
real.
Skill rusty? Watch someone great (or your past self on video) and mimic.
Fear blocking you? Visualize success in the mirror — trick your brain into
believing it'
s possible.
Reflection Point
What part of your life feels
"
paralyzed"? Set up a mirror (literal or mental). Watch
your strong side succeed. Imagine the weak side following. Do it daily for 10 minutes.
Your brain can be tricked into healing. Trick it.
Sasquatch’s Steel"Mirror therapy fools the brain — but the results are real. Watch the strong side win.
Make the weak side jealous. Then make it join the party. The Sasquatch didn
't wait
for movement — I created it. So can you.
"
Builder’s Note
A mirror, some imagination, and relentless reps — that'
s all it takes to start rewiring.
Don
't wait for the brain to catch up. Trick it into catching up. Keep swinging.<br
clear=

all"

Chapter 15: Conquering Drop Foot
and Reclaiming Your Stride / Drop
Foot DESTRUCTION — Walking Like a
Warrior Again
Drop foot was my daily enemy for years. Toes dragged, ankle flopped, every step
risked a fall. Doctors said "
permanent nerve damage.
" I said "temporary bullshit.
"
Here
'
s how I destroyed drop foot and reclaimed a natural stride.
Understanding Drop Foot
Peroneal nerve damage → weak anterior tibialis → foot drops → drag toes → trip →
fear → less walking → weaker muscles → worse drop foot. Vicious cycle.
I broke it with a multi-attack strategy.
My Drop Foot Destruction Arsenal
1.
Ankle Dorsiflexion Drills (100+ reps/day)○
Seated: Lift toes toward shin with band resistance.
○
Standing: Heel walks (walk on heels only).
2.
Negative Pressure / Eccentric Loading
○
Stand on step edge. Lower heels below step slowly (eccentric). Rise
onto toes (concentric).
○
Wall leans: Lean forward, press ankles into dorsiflexion under body
weight.
3.
Mirror Therapy + Mental Rehearsal
○
Watch good foot lift in mirror. Imagine left doing same. 10–20 min/day.
4.
AFO Ditching Protocol (Risky — Do Safely)
○
Started short walks without AFO (held wall/ Person
Michael).
○
Built confidence in ankle stability.
○
Gradual outdoor use. Never fell hard — adjusted when needed.
5. 66-Day Consistency Streak
○
Locked in daily reps for 66 days. Habit formed. Brain rewired. Foot lift
became automatic.
Results
●
●
●
Year 1–2: AFO required, dragging bad.
Year 3: Short walks without AFO possible.
Year 4+: Drop foot almost gone. Walk with little limp. Natural stride reclaimed.
Universal Lesson
Every
"drop foot" in life — bad habit, fear, weakness — can be destroyed the same
way: targeted drills, consistency, mental rehearsal, gradual exposure, refusal to
accept "
"
permanent.
Reflection Point
What'
s your
"drop foot"
— the thing dragging you down? Write it down. Pick one daily
drill (physical or mental). Commit to 66 days. Track it. Your heart'
s still thumpin
'
. Your
life is still pumpin
'
. Get knocked down five times, stand up six.
Sasquatch’s Steel"Drop foot ain
't permanent until you quit trying. Attack it daily. Rewire it. Destroy it.
The Sasquatch didn
't drag his foot forever — neither should you.
"
Builder’s Note
Drop foot destruction isn
't luck — it'
s reps, refusal, and system. Reclaim your stride.
Walk like a warrior again. Keep swinging.<br clear=

Motivation isn’t some fluffy feeling that shows up when the stars align. It’s a machine

burning rage — but rage burns out. What kept me going through years of flickers
