Chapter 21: Home Again: Adapting
to Your New Reality / Home Sweet
Home — Conquering Daily Life
Coming home from the hospital was supposed to be a victory lap. It felt like walking
into a war zone with no weapons. No nurses on call. No therapy schedule. Just me, a
wheelchair, dead weight left side, and a house that suddenly felt like it was built for
giants. Stairs became mountains. Doorways became choke points. Shower became
a potential drowning hazard. Kitchen counters became unreachable. I was a 250-lb
Arkansas boy turned prisoner in my own home — fully wheelchair-bound or crawling
on all fours like some kind of broken animal.
But home wasn
't the end of recovery. It was where the real fight started. Here
'
s how I
turned that house into my personal training ground and conquered daily life one
brutal, humiliating, victorious rep at a time.
The Home Reality Shock●
●
●
●
Mobility Hell: Slide board transfers to bed or toilet — one slip and I was on
the floor again.
Bathroom Wars: Raised toilet seat (thank God), grab bars in both showers
(my father installed them), shower chair at first. Sponge baths from
Person
Lisa. Catheter crystallized after two months — removal felt like fire.
Blood everywhere. I got front-row seats to the fight and passed out 10
seconds in. Good thing I love looking at my own blood so much or I might
have really passed out! /sarcasm
Kitchen Nightmare: Counters too high. Couldn
't reach anything. Pureed food
phase lingered. Appetite came roaring back — gained 80 pounds chillin
'
and
wheelin
'
. Beard hid the gobbler. Belt sighed in relief twice a day: once putting
pants on, once taking them off.
Isolation Peak: Friends vanished like smoke. Family fights over visitation
restrictions. Depression crushed me — laid in bed 24/7, smoked like a
chimney, fantasized about steering into traffic to end it. Felt like a burden
ruining everyone
'
s life.
My father stepped up big: installed a wheelchair ramp on the front porch so I could
get in and out without being carried. Put handrails in both showers. Raised toilet
seat. Shower chair. He didn
't say much — just built. That quiet action spoke louder
than any pep talk.
The Turning Point — Fatherhood Fire
My son was born while I was still wheelchair-bound. Couldn
't even stand for the
delivery. Watched from the chair as his little pale body poked up over Person
Lisa
'
s horizon. That moment hit like a freight train: I was a father. But what kind of
father could I be stuck seated? How could I play catch, teach him to throw a spiral,
show him how to be a man — from a chair?
I made a promise right there:
"I'll give everything to be the best father possible. I'll
play catch with both arms.
" That why pulled me out of bed when nothing else could.
Purpose > pity. Fatherhood became my ultimate motivation — the fire that turned
home from prison to proving ground.Home Adaptation Arsenal
1.
2.
3.
4.
5.
Ramp & Transfer Mastery Father

s ramp = freedom to leave house. Slide
board practice daily — from embarrassing falls to smooth transfers.
Bathroom Conquest Raised seat + grab bars = dignity. Shower chair → bench
→ standing (with handrail). No more sponge baths. Independence won.
Kitchen Takeover Reacher tool at first. One-handed cooking drills. Grip reps
built strength. Ate for recovery — high protein, BDNF boosters (eggs, nuts,
fish). Weight gain? Fuel for muscle rebuild.
Daily Life Reps Brushing teeth, buttoning shirts, opening doors — forced
functional tasks. Every struggle = rep. TikTok filming = accountability + tribe.
Mindset Shift Home wasn
't prison — it was gym. Every task a drill. Every
humiliation a story. Every small win proof:
"I'
m still thumpin
'
. Life
'
s still pumpin
'
"

't just help — she showed
compassion, companionship, friendship. Swept my floor, sure — but more

After physical recovery stabilized (around 50-60% function), the mental struggle
began: What now? Disability checks covered basics, but purpose starved. The old
corporate ladder was inaccessible. My new physical limits demanded a new
professional path.

Recovery Academy (SRA).

impossible is possible.

The Medium: TikTok (raw, real videos).

Monetize the Impact: Turning the recovery knowledge into revenue. The
money fuels the mission: more survivors helped, more tools created.
90% and Climbing

't done.

About the Author
Nicholas
"Stroked-Out Sasquatch" Kremers is a stroke survivor, entrepreneur, and
the founder of the Stroke Recovery Academy. On December 3, 2018, he suffered a
massive hemorrhagic stroke that left him with severe left-side paralysis. Doctors
predicted a lifetime of limited function. He chose to prove them wrong. Over the
next seven years, through relentless experimentation, high-intensity training, and an
unbreakable mindset, he achieved over 90% functional recovery and started the
StrokeLyfe movement on TikTok and Facebook to teach others his
"battle-tested
blueprint.
" He lives in Place
Fort Smith, Arkansas, where he continues to train,
build his empire, and inspire thousands to believe that recovery never stops.<br

'

throughout life. Drives

PART I: THE STORM — THE
BREAKDOWN TO BREAKTHROUGH
Chapter 1: The Day Everything Changes
/ December 3, 2018 — The Day I
Refused to Die
One moment I was built like a brick wall — benching over four hundred pounds, working
long days, feeling unbreakable. Then, the next, half my body went silent. The lights didn’t
go out completely that day; they just flickered. My left side stopped listening. My arm
hung heavy, my leg dead weight, words crumbling on my tongue.
I tried to tell myself it was exhaustion, dehydration — anything but what it was. But the
truth hit like a sledgehammer: I’d had a massive hemorrhagic stroke.
The Body That Betrayed Me
I’d always been the strong one — the athlete, the protector, the worker. Football,
basketball, baseball, gymnastics as a kid. If it took grit, I was in.
So when my body quit, it wasn’t just medical. It was an identity collapse. How does a man
built on strength function when strength is gone? Lying in that hospital bed, I wasn’t
thinking about recovery or inspiration. I was thinking about survival — and the terrifying
thought that my own body had become a prison.
The Chainsaw in My SkullThe pain wasn’t what broke me — it was the confusion. It felt like chainsaws buzzed
behind my eyes. I even reached up once, sure I’d find steel under my scalp. Every beep of
a monitor mocked me. I couldn’t fix this with muscle or willpower. The same body that
once obeyed every command now ignored me completely.
And then came the humiliation. In the ICU, I lost 50 pounds — down to tube feedings, then
pureed foods that tasted like cardboard mush. Doctors said the stroke hit areas that would
wipe out music recognition — I’d never know a Britney Spears song from a chainsaw
again, couldn’t even tell a hot dog from a burger by sight or sound. Boy were they wrong!
One day, while dealing with the straight-up embarrassment of pooping myself
(incontinence hitting hard from the brain damage),
"Oops... I Did It Again" blasted in my
head. Not just blasting — I sang it out loud, as loud as I could, which was a damn feat
considering I had just graduated from tubes to purees. Nurses probably thought I was
losing it, but it was proof: music was still there, appetite intact, brain still firing in ways
they said it wouldn’t.
When I got home, it flipped — gained 80 pounds chillin' and wheelin' in my wheelchair. I
grew a beard to hide the gobbler that developed. My belt sighs in relief twice a day: once
when I put my pants on, and again when they come off at night! The weight scale wishes
the docs had locked that one down as fact.
That’s when I realized: recovery wasn’t going to be about strength. It was going to be
about communication — and hope.Builder’s Science: The First Signal
After a stroke, the brain’s communication network misfires — neurons lose connection to
the body’s "blueprints.
" But under the right conditions, neuroplasticity kicks in: new
pathways form, rerouting messages through surviving tissue. It’s like rewiring a circuit
board after an explosion — slow, delicate, but possible.
Every attempt to move, even a failed one, sends sparks through that rewiring process.
Every flicker counts.
(Reference: Kleim & Jones, 2008; NIH Neurorehab Review, 2021)
The Moment Everything Changed
When I met Michael, my physical therapist, he didn’t look like much — tall, lanky,
unassuming. But he carried belief like a toolbelt.
He didn’t baby me. He didn’t promise miracles. He just said,
Nick. We’re here to rebuild.
"
"We’re not here to survive,
That was the first flicker — not in my muscles, but in my mindset. The idea that maybe,
somehow, I could build again.
From Athlete to PatientMy new sport was standing. My new gym, a hallway lined with parallel bars. Each step
was a victory; each failure was data.
Michael would say,
repetition. Repetition meant growth.
"Don’t think of it as exercise — think of it as practice.
" Practice meant
He had me chase balloons to retrain balance, climb stairs with assistance, and use tools
no textbook mentioned. Every creative challenge sent a new signal to my brain. It wasn’t
about muscle — it was about rewiring.
That’s the thing about recovery: you don’t rebuild by routine. You rebuild by
problem-solving under pressure — one flicker at a time.
The First Flicker
The day my left hand twitched, it didn’t look like much. But to me, it was a miracle. A
spark of life inside a body I’d already grieved. I stared at that twitch like a mechanic
watching a dead engine fire once, just once, after months of silence.
That was the first time I understood what progress really was. Not leaps. Flickers. Not
miracles. Messages.
That tiny movement proved that the lights might dim — but they never truly go out.
Reflection Point
What was your "flicker" moment — the first tiny sign that your body or mind was still
fighting back?
How did it feel? Write it down. That flicker is proof your heart is still thumpin'
life is still pumpin'
. Celebrate it today — no matter how small.
— so your
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Chop wood when you're tired. Stand even when standing hurts. Celebrate flickers as if
they were fireworks. Hope is contagious — without it, I'd have started digging my own
grave years ago. Everyone needs hope and a purpose. Find yours today.
"
Builder’s Note
The lights may flicker, but they never go out if you keep swinging.Chapter 2: ICU Hell — Where I Learned
to Fight Back / The Two Paths:
Limitation or Liberation
The ICU wasn't just a room — it was hell with beeps and fluorescent lights. It was a
month-long stay that nearly broke me. Morphine turned my brain into a funhouse mirror
— hallucinations where nurses were plotting against me, people who weren't there,
feeling trapped in some medical experiment. Medical explanation: brain swelling and
meds. Real explanation: your own mind turning against you when you need it most.
The Communication & Swallowing Nightmare
Words wouldn't come. My thoughts clear in my head, but my mouth refused to cooperate.
Swallowing? Gone. I started on tube feedings — that damn tube down my throat. One
night I woke up choking on it. Lisa freaked out, yelling for help. Response was slow. She
didn't wait — ripped that tube out like a boss. Blood, chaos, nurses rushing in. We might
not have a romantic relationship anymore, but we're still a team raising our son. That
moment? Pure warrior mode from her.After tubes came pureed food — tasted like baby mush, took an hour to swallow crushed
meds in applesauce. Doctors said the stroke location meant I'd lose music recognition,
and couldn't tell a hot dog from a burger. Wrong again. Britney blasted through the pain,
and the appetite survived.
The Dependency & Humiliation Hits
Everything taken for granted became impossible: rolling over, reaching water, adjusting
the blanket, scratching an itch, bathroom dignity. The psychological hit was brutal —
suddenly dependent on others for basic human stuff. This destroys self-worth. But it also
teaches you to fight for independence like hell.
One day, coming back from rehab, I had to use a slide board to transfer from the
wheelchair to the bed. Mobility was trash. I slipped and fell hard on the tile floor. Couldn't
move. Three people tried lifting me — no chance. They finally called in the Hoyer lift —
hoisting me up like a 20k-pound I-beam on a skyscraper. Degrading. Embarrassing.
Nothing I could do.
Then the catheter story. Two months with that thing in — supposed to be changed and
cleaned regularly. Nope. Crystallized to my member. When discharge came, the nurse
removed it. Hurt like the dickens. Blood dripped down my leg, on the floor. I got front-row
seats to the fight — knocked out cold 10 seconds in.
Lisa's Loyalty During the Chaos
Lisa was pregnant through all this. She rarely left my side — only to shower. Weeks after
the stroke, she found out it was a boy. Our baby boy. She stayed. Held my pee cup, gave
sponge baths, fought the system. Thank you, Lisa — for our son, for being there when
everything was falling apart.
The Darkest Thoughts
Brutal honesty: I wondered if everyone would be better off if I didn't make it. Burden. No
independence. The best days behind me. Depression after stroke is real — it can kill
recovery before it starts. Those thoughts are normal. Dangerous. You've gotta fight them
with purpose.
The Hardest, Most Unbearable, Slow, Yet Uber Rewarding Experience
This stroke has been the hardest, most unbearable, slow grind of my life. Yet it's also the
most rewarding. Forced me to rebuild everything — body, mind, purpose. Taught me hopeis contagious. Purpose is non-negotiable. Without it, I'd have dug my own grave years
ago.
The Two Paths: Limitation or Liberation
The ICU is where you face the ultimate choice: Limitation or Liberation.
●
●
Limitation is accepting the doctor's statistics, feeling guilt, and letting dependency
destroy your fight.
Liberation is using the humiliation and the darkest thoughts as fuel, fighting for

bigger than your pain.
The gap between impossible and inevitable is your refusal to quit. Your hell is your
launching pad.

Chapter 3: The Compound Effect of
Choosing Possible
Rock bottom ain't the end — it's the cleanest slate you'll ever get. When everything's
stripped away, there's no clutter, no excuses, no "what ifs" left to hide behind. I hit it hard
in that ICU bed — 50 staples in my skull, left side paralyzed, mind spinning from
hallucinations, body betraying me at every turn. Doctors whispering "permanent,
" family
fighting over visitation, me wondering if the people I loved would be better off without theburden. That's rock bottom. But here's the truth nobody tells you: rock bottom is the most
solid foundation you can build on. Nothing left to lose means everything left to gain.
The Choice That Defines Everything
Every morning in recovery, you wake up and make a choice that decides your whole
future. Most people don't even realize they're making it — it happens on autopilot. But
once you see it clearly, you take control.
The choice is simple:
Probable or Possible?
Probable is what statistics and doctors sell you:
●
●
●
●
●
Most recover 60–70% at best.
Big gains stop after 6–12 months.
Significant improvement unlikely after 2 years.
Adjust expectations to avoid disappointment.
Be "realistic"
— manage limitations, accept the new normal.
Possible is what happens when you refuse the sales pitch:
●
What if I push beyond the timeline?●
●
●
What if I experiment until something clicks?
What if I treat "never" as a dare?
What if my brain rewires farther than anyone predicts?
Probable feels safe — predictable, comfortable, backed by experts. But it costs you
everything: your true potential, breakthrough opportunities, the chance to become proof
that the rules were incomplete.
I made my choice in that hospital bed. A doctor looked at my scans and said,
"You'll
probably never walk normally again. We need to focus on helping you adapt.
" In that
instant, I could have nodded, accepted it, learned to live small.
Instead, I treated it as one person's best guess based on limited data. I chose possible.
"Watch me.
"
The Rebellion Against "Never"
When someone says "never,
" what they're really saying is:
●
●
●
●
"I don't know how.
"
"It's statistically unlikely.

They're not saying:
●
●
●
●
"It's impossible.
"
"Your brain can't change.
"
"You should stop trying.
"
"Miracles don't happen.

My answer to every "never"? Watch me prove it wrong.
The Science of Belief
Research backs this up hard:
●
●
Patients who believe recovery is possible recover more function (positive
expectations increase therapy compliance).
Hope and optimism improve neuroplasticity (belief drives BDNF release,
strengthens new pathways).●
Belief in continued improvement leads to continued improvement (self-fulfilling
prophecy in the right direction).
Your mind isn't along for the ride — it's driving the bus. Choose possible every day, and
the bus goes farther.
My Daily Choice Ritual
Every morning I did the same thing:
Instead of "What can't I do today?" → "What might be possible today?"
Instead of focusing on losses → Focused on potential.
Instead of "This is as good as it gets" → "This is where the real work begins.

Those small daily choices compounded. Day 1: Try moving a finger instead of accepting

Chapter 5: The First Build Site — From
ICU Survivor to Rehab Warrior
You survived the initial blast. Now, the real construction begins. Leaving the ICU is like
getting airlifted out of a war zone—there is relief, but a new, completely different fight
awaits. This isn't about staying alive anymore; it’s about re-learning how to live.
My first stop: Encompass Health, the first of eight inpatient rehab stays. That statistic
alone tells you everything you need to know about the long-haul commitment required.
The ICU was about flickers of life; rehab is about the unglamorous, repetitive, day-in,
day-out grind. This is where you trade the life support machine for a hard hat and a tool
belt.The Shock of Repetition
In the ICU, every tiny movement was a miracle. In rehab, every tiny movement is a
mandatory repetition. The environment changed from an emergency room to a
construction site.
●
●
ICU Focus (Survival Phase): Stabilize, survive, and keep the lights on.
Rehab Focus (Construction Phase): Repetition, practice, and rebuilding the body’s
blueprint from scratch.
It was here I learned the difference between healing (passive, time-based) and
construction (active, effort-based). Healing happens to you. Construction you do yourself.
The first few weeks were a psychological shock: endless transfers, balance drills, and the
soul-crushing weight of knowing this wasn’t a sprint. It was a marathon where every mile
was uphill. The biggest trap in rehab is letting the routine become a rut.
The Law of Forced Growth
The most painful tool in the rehab arsenal was the parallel bars. I stood there, channeling
all my focus into making my left leg step forward. It was a war of nerves. My brain sent
the signal—Move!—but the leg remained dead weight, dragging and scraping the floor.
One session, trying a drill Michael called the "Step-Tap-Hold,
" I stumbled, caught myself
on the bars, and swore out loud. Michael, who always knew the score, just said: "Yourbrain is not broken, Nick. It’s rerouting. It needs the map. You have to draw that map with
sweat.
"
That’s the Law of Forced Growth: You have to force the brain to fire new pathways
through relentless, specific, and often frustrating repetition. You can't just wait for the
power to come back on; you have to rewire the house. Discomfort is not the enemy;
comfortable is the enemy.
The Hidden Victory: Community
The ICU was isolating. Rehab was the first time I was surrounded by other
warriors—stroke survivors and traumatic brain injury patients who understood the
"chainsaw in the skull" feeling. We shared the dark humor, the tiny wins (like feeding
yourself without spilling), and the absolute certainty that the person next to you was
fighting the same invisible war.
This community became the first external component of the Sasquatch Armor. Hope is
contagious. You see a survivor walking, and a voice in your head screams,
"If they can do
it, watch me.
" Rehab wasn't just therapy; it was the boot camp where I learned to use
frustration as fuel. It’s where I started transforming from a patient into the architect of my
own future.
Builder’s Blueprint: Early Rehab Focus
1.
2.
3.
Ditch the "Patient" Mindset: You're not sick; you're in training. This is a job. Show
up and punch the clock.
Embrace the Boring: Breakthroughs are built on the back of monotonous
repetition. Master the transfer. Master the single step. The basics are the
foundation.
Find Your Tribe: Connect with other survivors. They are proof of possibility. Their
wins are your fuel.
Reflection Point
What is the most tedious, boring, but absolutely essential repetition you need to commit to
today? Is it 10 more reps? A few extra minutes of standing? Write it down. That boring
work is the steel rebar in your new foundation. Now go do it.

"The miracle isn't the first flicker. The miracle is showing up for the millionth rep. Chop
wood. Build the wall. The only person who says it's impossible is the one who quit.
"Builder’s Note
Rehab isn't a parking lot; it's a construction site. Punch the clock, swing the hammer, and

PART II: THE CURRICULUM — THE
SCIENCE OF IMPOSSIBLE RECOVERY
Chapter 6: Your Brain's Incredible
Healing Power / Your Recovery Machine
— The Brain That Never Quits
Your brain isn't broken — it's a machine that's been damaged but still has the power to
rebuild itself. The stroke hit hard, carved a path of destruction through my left
hemisphere, left me paralyzed on one side, struggling to speak, swallow, think straight.
Doctors said "extensive damage,
" "permanent deficits,
" "plateau after year one.
" They
were looking at averages. I was looking at possibility.The Brain That Refuses to Quit
Neuroplasticity is the brain's ability to reorganize itself by forming new neural connections
throughout life. After stroke, undamaged areas can take over lost functions. New
pathways form. Old ones strengthen. It's not magic — it's mechanics. Your brain is
designed to adapt, survive, thrive.
When I first tried to move my left hand, nothing. Zero. But every attempt, every flicker,
every failed rep sent a signal. The brain listened. It started building bridges around the
damage. Slow. Painful. Invisible for months. But it never stopped.
Builder’s Science: How Rewiring Actually Works
●
●
●
●
●
BDNF (Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor): Exercise, repetition, novelty release
this "fertilizer" for neurons. More BDNF = more growth, stronger connections.
Hebb's Law: "Neurons that fire together wire together.
" Every time you attempt a
movement (even if it fails), neurons fire. Repeat enough, they wire new pathways.
Use It or Lose It: Idle areas weaken. Forced use (therapy, daily reps) strengthens
them.
Bilateral Training: Strong side teaches weak side. Mirror therapy tricks the brain
into seeing movement on the paralyzed side.
Intensity Matters: Low-effort therapy gives low results. High-rep, high-focus work
rewires faster.I didn't wait for the brain to heal itself. I attacked it with reps, tools, experiments. The brain
responded — not because it had to, but because I gave it no choice.
My Proof: From 0% to 90%+
●
●
●
●
Year 1: Flickers, tiny movements, first assisted steps.
Year 2–3: Walking without AFO, basic arm use.
Year 4–5: Strength gains, bilateral coordination, speech clarity.
Year 6–7: Memory doubled again, athletic movements, still rising.
Doctors said plateau after year one. I said "watch me.
" The brain kept rewiring because I
kept feeding it signals.
Universal Truth: Any Brain Can Adapt
This isn't just for stroke. Any setback — addiction, trauma, depression, business failure —
damages neural pathways. Same rules apply: starve the bad habits, feed the new ones
with repetition, intensity, novelty. The brain rewires. Always.
Reflection Point
What "never" has your brain been told? Write it down. Then write one small daily action
you can take to send a new signal. Do it today. Your brain is listening. Feed it the right
input. Watch it rebuild.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Your brain isn't broken — it's waiting for orders. Give it reps, give it purpose, give it no
choice but to rewire. The Sasquatch didn't quit. Neither should you.
"
Builder’s Note
The brain that survives doesn't just adapt — it transcends. What didn't kill your neural
network makes it exponentially stronger. Keep feeding it signals. Keep swinging.Chapter 7: Neuroplasticity: The Science
of the Impossible / Neuroplasticity
Revolution — Recovery NEVER Stops
The doctors gave you a timeline. They whispered things like "plateau" and "what you get
in the first year is mostly it.
" They meant well. They were wrong. They were looking at the
average patient who stops swinging the hammer. You are not average. You are a warrior
enrolled at Sasquatch Recovery University, and here, we recognize one truth: Your brain
is still on the clock.
Neuroplasticity is the brain’s ability to reorganize itself by forming new neural
connections. It’s not a one-time event that shuts off after 12 months; it’s a living,
breathing, constantly updating construction site. Every time you try to move a weak limb,
every time you practice a difficult word, you send an electrical jolt through the damaged
circuitry. If the signal finds an alternative route, it fires. Do it again. And again. The path
that was a muddy goat track on Day 1 becomes a paved freeway on Day 1,000.
The Builder’s Law of Forced Input
The brain will only rewire what you force it to use. It’s a resource manager. Why build a
new bridge if the old one isn't being used? You need three things to force growth:1.
2.
3.
Intensity: Low-effort, passive movements (like a few gentle stretches) are a waste
of time. Your brain needs high-rep, high-focus work to trigger the growth factor
(BDNF) that acts as the soil for new neural pathways.
Repetition: The goal is not 10 reps a day; the goal is 100 or 1,000. Do the
movement until the signal is no longer a whisper but a scream. Monotony is the
mother of all breakthroughs.
Novelty: Once a movement becomes easy, change the task. Stand on a different
surface. Use a weight. Close your eyes. The brain learns best when it’s forced to
problem-solve under pressure.
The Proof: I was told my biggest gains would be in year one. My biggest, most
jaw-dropping wins—like walking without the AFO, doubling my memory processing, and
building the Stroke Recovery Academy—happened in years 4, 5, 6, and 7. The recovery
never stopped because I never stopped feeding it input. The timeline is a suggestion for
quitters. For us, recovery is infinite.
Reflection Point
What is the biggest "never" you've been told about your body's limits? Write it down.
Now, find one highly repetitive, boring movement (like lifting a finger or tapping your toe)
you can do 100 times today. That's your first wire.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"The brain is the ultimate architect. It will build anything you ask it to—but you have to
deliver the steel, the blueprints, and the relentless labor. Never let the clock decide when
the work stops.
"
Builder’s Note
Neuroplasticity is your most powerful tool. Treat every single rep like a new hammer
swing on the damaged circuit board. The longer you swing, the stronger the connection
gets.Chapter 8: Finding My Way: From
Wheelchair to Walking — How I Taught
Myself to Walk and Almost Totally
Cured My Drop Foot
When I left the ICU, walking was an embarrassing stumble. My left foot, dead weight from
the knee down, was a hazard, not a tool. Drop foot is the enemy of liberation; it locks you
back in the wheelchair or to the safety of a cane. Doctors offered the Ankle-Foot Orthosis
(AFO) as a permanent solution. I saw it as a temporary training wheel. This chapter is the
demolition plan for drop foot.
The Blueprint: Deconstruction and Rebuild
The core of conquering drop foot is simple: the anterior tibialis muscle, which lifts your
foot, is weak, and the brain has forgotten its command signal. You must re-establish the
connection through three specific, forced steps:
1.
Isolated Signal Fire: Start non-weight-bearing. Sit and concentrate on wiggling
your left toes and lifting your whole foot. Try to twitch the muscle. Do this until it
burns. The sheer act of intention is the signal.2.
3.
Forced Lengthening (Negative Pressure): As covered in Chapter 13, I used the
Negative Pressure Therapy hack. Stand (assisted!) and slowly lean forward,
putting maximum downward pressure on the front of the ankle and foot. This
forces the muscle to activate eccentrically (while lengthening). It hurts like hell. It
works like a miracle.
The Step-Tap-Hold Drill: Michael's genius. In the parallel bars, shift your weight
onto your strong side. Now, with your weak leg, lift the foot just enough to clear the
ground, tap the heel, and hold the foot up for a full second before placing it
forward. The hold is the game-changer. It trains the anterior tibialis to fire under
load.
The Proof: For years, I did these drills hundreds of times a day. I taped weights to my foot
for resistance. I walked on grass, then pavement, then hills. I was obsessed with that
'hold' command. Today, I walk without an AFO. I’ve gone from shuffling to a confident,
athletic stride. The nerve damage didn't disappear, but the brain found a new, superior
highway.
Reflection Point
If your focus is on the big goal of "running again,
" you'll fail. Focus on the tiny goal of a
"perfect toe clearance.
" Pick one drill from above and commit to 50 repetitions every day
this week. The details of the step build the path to the stride.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Drop foot isn't permanent nerve damage—it's temporary lack of input. You are the one
who delivers the command. Never stop fighting for your stride. A warrior doesn't shuffle;
a warrior walks with purpose.
"
Builder’s Note
Don't wait for your brain to give you a natural gait. You have to build the gait, rep by rep,
until your brain accepts it as the new default setting.Chapter 9: The 66-Day Transformation
— How Consistency Rewires Everything
You can have the greatest blueprint in the world (this book), the fiercest resolve, and the
best physical therapist (your Michael), but if you are not consistent, you will fail. The
66-Day Transformation is the non-negotiable construction schedule. It’s based on a
simple truth: it takes an average of 66 days for a new behavior to become automatic. Not
21 days, not 30. Sixty-six.
The Blueprint: From Intent to Inevitable
The 66-Day Transformation is a non-stop, 24/7 commitment to a single, measurable
recovery habit.
1.
2.
Identify ONE Keystone Habit: Don't choose five things. Choose one. Example: "I
will do 100 reps of isolated hand-opening per day,
" or "I will stand unaided for a
total of 15 minutes a day.
"
The Non-Negotiable Rule: You must complete the habit for 66 straight days. If you
miss one day, you go back to Day 1. There is no negotiating with the schedule. This
is how you prove to your brain that this new habit is more important than the old
pattern (learned non-use).3.
The Anchor: Anchor the habit to an existing routine. Example: "I will do my 100
reps immediately after my morning coffee,
" or "I will do my standing practice while
watching the 6 o'clock news.
" This removes the need for willpower.
The Proof: The 66-Day commitment is what took me from twitches to functional
movement. I started with standing for one minute. Day 1 was brutal. Day 66, it was
automatic. I ran multiple 66-Day programs back-to-back: one for walking, one for hand
opening, one for speech clarity. Consistency is the compound effect of recovery—small,
daily choices that lead to massive, inevitable breakthroughs.
Reflection Point
What is the one, single, measurable habit that would unlock your next phase of recovery?
Write it down. Get your 66-Day Tracker (Appendix G) and mark today as Day 1. If you miss
tomorrow, you fail. Start over. Don't let your old self win.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"The brain is a stubborn contractor. It only builds the foundation when the work shows up
every single day. Consistency is the bridge between possible and inevitable. Show up for
66 days or start digging a grave for your potential.
"
Builder’s Note
The 66-Day Transformation forces the neural pathways to become permanent. It’s not
about intensity here; it’s about persistence.
Chapter 10: The Kremers Recovery
Formula / The Sasquatch Formula —
TAKE→LEARN→EXPERIMENT→COMBI
NE→APPLY
Recovery isn't a medical textbook—it's a massive, chaotic engineering problem. The
therapists before Michael were stuck in the "prescription" mindset: do this, no more, noless. Michael and I were in the "experimentation" mindset. You need a system for this, and
this is the formula forged on the floor of the rehab gym.
The Sasquatch Formula (T-L-E-C-A): Your Operating System
1.
TAKE (The Input): Start with whatever you can get. Take the advice from a doctor,
a snippet from a study, a drill from a therapist, or an idea from a fellow survivor.
Don't filter, just take the raw material.
2.
LEARN (The Science): Why did they tell you to do it? Read the study. Ask your
therapist. Understand the muscle, the nerve, the neuroplastic principle at play. You
cannot fix the engine without knowing the mechanics.
3.
EXPERIMENT (The Lab): This is where you become the architect. Take the input
and modify it. Change the angle. Add a weight. Increase the speed. Do it standing.
Do it sitting. My Negative Pressure Therapy (Chapter 13) and Baseball Bat Therapy
(Chapter 12) were born here. The official way failed. So I invented a better way.
4. COMBINE (The Blueprint): Mix the successful experiments. Combine mirror
therapy with a movement drill. Combine weight-bearing with an external stim
device. The combination of successful micro-hacks is what creates the true
breakthrough.
5.
APPLY (The Scale): Once you have a successful combination, you execute the
66-Day Transformation (Chapter 9). This is the scaling phase—the monotonous,
unrelenting application that makes the breakthrough permanent.The Proof: Doctors told me my drop foot was permanent (TAKE). I read studies on
eccentric loading and forced dorsiflexion (LEARN). I started leaning against a bar, forcing
the weak muscle to fight against gravity and my own weight (EXPERIMENT). I combined
that with the Step-Tap-Hold drill (COMBINE). I did this for 66 days straight, then 66 more
(APPLY). Result? No AFO.
Reflection Point
What one piece of standard, boring therapy are you doing today? Now, apply
EXPERIMENT. How can you change it to make it 10x harder, more specific, or more
focused on your current goal? That uncomfortable change is where the growth is hiding.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"You are not a patient; you are a Chief Engineer. Never follow a blueprint blindly. Question
the methods, embrace the chaos of the experiment, and use every failure as data to
invent your next breakthrough.
"
Builder’s Note
The formula is your roadmap to extraordinary results. It turns information into knowledge,
and knowledge into power.
Chapter 11: Think-Twitch-Move: The
Core Progression /
Think-Flinch-Existence — From
Paralysis to Power
The ultimate humiliation of the stroke is the disconnect between your mind and your body.
The mind screams,
"MOVE!" and the limb replies with absolute, dead silence. This gap is
the battlefield of the Think-Flinch-Existence progression—a deep-brain hack that restores
the primal connection.The Blueprint: Re-establishing Command
This progression is about brute-forcing the signal to the brainstem, bypassing the
damaged cerebral cortex until new pathways are strong enough.
1.
2.
3.
Think (The Intention): Close your eyes. Place 100% of your focus on the single,
smallest movement you want. Don't just wish for it—send a white-hot, singular
command to that limb. Think-Twitch.
Flinch (The Breakthrough): A flinch is the raw, unplanned, involuntary movement
that precedes a controlled voluntary one. It’s a cough, a sneeze, a shock, or a
sudden, explosive effort that causes a flicker of movement. When you get a flinch,
celebrate it like a firework. This is the brain proving the line is open. Immediately
after, try the Think again.
Existence (The Default): This is the phase where the limb starts to activate under
load, during daily life, without conscious effort. The limb exists in your active
world. You can scratch an itch. You can use it to stabilize. The connection is
permanent; the movement is becoming automatic.
The Proof: My first flicker (Chapter 1) was a small twitch in my left hand. I used that twitch
as the foundation. For weeks, I would Think and try to replicate that twitch. I would Think
about moving my left hand while driving, while talking, while eating. Slowly, the twitches
turned into tiny, unreliable movements. Now, my hand fires automatically for balance and
grip. I went from the Think phase (total conscious effort) through the Flinch phase
(involuntary response) to the Existence phase (automatic function).Reflection Point
Stop trying to move your hand or foot by moving your hand or foot. Sit in total silence and
focus only on the signal. Spend one minute trying to generate a flinch—that white-hot
command. Did you feel anything? A tingle? A warmth? A whisper? That’s the brain
listening. Write it down.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Paralysis is not silence; it is a broken speaker. The mind is still shouting the command.
Your job is to replace the wiring, one tiny pulse at a time. The flinch is proof you are
winning the war for control.
"
Builder’s Note
This is the mental warfare section. The intention is the first movement. The first flicker of
life in the limb is a direct result of relentless, focused intention.
PART III: THE ARSENAL —
BREAKTHROUGH TECHNIQUES THAT
ACTUALLY WORK
Chapter 12: First Steps: Small Victories
Build Big Comebacks / Baseball Bat
Therapy — My Secret Weapon
Most therapists avoid spasticity (stiffness/contractures) because it is hard to manage.
They tell you to stretch gently. I call bulls***
. Spasticity is a coiled spring—it’s bad tone,
but it’s still tone. My goal was to turn that bad tone into functional power. Baseball Bat
Therapy was my first, most successful, and most controversial experiment.The Blueprint: From Spasm to Function
WARNING: This is a high-intensity, un-prescribed, self-administered technique.
CONSULT YOUR PHYSICIAN/THERAPIST BEFORE ATTEMPTING.
The goal is to force the spastic muscles (flexors) to relax by overwhelming the opposing
muscles (extensors) with ballistic, highly resistive movement.
1.
2.
3.
4.
The Tool: I used a standard, heavy wooden baseball bat.
The Setup: With a therapist (Michael) spotting me, I would grip the bat with my
strong right hand. My weak left hand would grip as much as it could (it was mostly
spastic flexion).
The Drill (Controlled Swing): I would execute a slow, highly focused two-handed
swing motion. The left arm had no choice but to follow. The resistance of the bat
forced the flexors to let go so the extensors could try to complete the movement.
The Result: The spasticity in my left arm would temporarily shatter. I’d have a
5-minute window of semi-controlled movement immediately after the session. I
used that window for highly focused hand/arm drills.
The Proof: This was the first technique that unlocked my left arm. The bat was too heavy
and the movement too fast for my spasticity to win. It was a neurological circuit breaker. It
broke the lock. It was painful, exhausting, and borderline reckless, but it gave me my firstmoments of controlled, functional movement in my arm. I would not have functional hand
use today without the Baseball Bat.
Reflection Point
What is the biggest "no" you get from your body? If it's a tight fist, the answer isn't gentle
stretching. It's safe, forced, repetitive counter-movement. You need to find your own
metaphorical 'bat'
—a tool or exercise that safely forces the weak side to submit to a
high-intensity command.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Spasticity is a wall of concrete. You don’t ask it nicely to move—you bring a wrecking
ball. The fear of pain is less damaging than the paralysis of learned non-use. Fight fire
with fire, but always have a spotter.
"
Builder’s Note
Find the safe line between intense training and injury. The most significant progress lives
right there, at the edge of your comfort zone. Comfortable is the enemy of recovered.
Chapter 13: Weight Bearing & Negative
Pressure Therapy / Weight Bearing
Revolution — Standing Your Way to
Strength
Weight bearing isn't just exercise — it's medicine. After the stroke, my left leg was dead
weight. No tone, no control, no hope from most therapists. They said "use the AFO
forever" and "don't put too much pressure on it — you might hurt yourself.
" I heard "stay
weak.
" I chose to stand anyway.Why Weight Bearing Works
Standing loads the bones, muscles, joints, and nervous system. It sends signals upward:
"Hey brain — this leg still exists. Wake it up.
" Gravity + body weight + intention =
neuroplasticity on steroids.
●
●
●
●
●
Bones get stronger (Wolff's Law — bone remodels under stress).
Muscles fire against gravity (anti-gravity extensors activate).
Proprioception rebuilds (brain relearns where the leg is in space).
Circulation improves (no more pooling blood in a limp limb).
Brain gets massive input — every second standing is thousands of neural signals.
I started with parallel bars and Michael's shoulder. Ten seconds felt like ten hours. But
every second counted. The brain was listening.
Negative Pressure Therapy — My Secret Hack
This was my own twist — using controlled "negative" (downward) pressure to force
activation.
How I did it:
●
●
Stood against a wall or bar.
Leaned forward slowly so body weight pressed into ankles/feet.●
●
●
Held 30–60 seconds — felt the stretch in calves, anterior tibialis fighting to lift toes.
Repeated 10–20 times per session.
Progressed: less wall support, longer holds, added small weights.
Why it worked:
●
●
●
●
Eccentric loading strengthens muscles during lengthening (most stroke therapies
focus only on concentric).
Stretches spastic muscles safely.
Forces dorsiflexion under load — directly attacks drop foot.
Builds confidence — every hold proved "this leg can support me.
"
Combined with weight bearing, this became my revolution. From zero to standing
independently. From dragging to walking. From AFO to freedom.
Safety First — But Don't Baby Yourself
Listen to your body. Start assisted. Build slowly. But don't let fear stop progress. Pain =
injury? Stop. Discomfort = growth? Keep going (safely).
Universal Application
Weight bearing isn't just for stroke legs.
●
Business feeling shaky? Put weight on it — take real risks, lean in, feel the
●
●
pressure.
Relationship falling apart? Stand in the discomfort — face the hard talks.
Health slipping? Put weight on better habits — stand against gravity every day.
Reflection Point
Where in your life are you avoiding weight? Write it down. Set one small "stand" this week
(literal or figurative). Feel the pressure. Let it build strength. Your heart's still thumpin'
—
so your life is still pumpin'
. Get knocked down five times, stand up six.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Stand even when standing hurts. Weight bearing isn't punishment — it's medicine. Lean
into the pressure. The Sasquatch didn't stay seated. Neither should you.
"
Builder’s NoteWeight bearing turns dead weight into living strength. Don't wait for permission. Stand.
Feel gravity. Rewire your brain. Keep swinging.
Chapter 14: Mirror Therapy Magic —
Tricking Your Brain to Heal
Mirror therapy was one of the most powerful, stupidly simple tools I used. A mirror. A box.
Some duct tape. And a brain that can be fooled into rewiring itself.
How It Works
●
●
●
●
Place mirror vertically between arms/legs.
Good side in front of mirror, paralyzed side hidden behind.
Move good side — watch reflection as if paralyzed side is moving.
Brain sees "movement" on bad side → mirror neurons fire → damaged pathways
get stimulated.
I did this 10–20 minutes daily. Watched my right hand open/close, imagined left doing
same. Felt weird at first — like lying to my brain. But it wasn't a lie. It was training.My Results
●
●
●
●
Early weeks: Nothing visible, but I felt tingles, warmth in left hand.
Month 2–3: Small twitches when mirroring.
Month 4+: Left hand started opening on its own during sessions.
Long term: Bilateral coordination improved dramatically.
Science backs it: Mirror therapy activates mirror neuron system, increases excitability in
damaged motor cortex, reduces learned non-use. It's cheap, safe, and stupidly effective.
Universal Application
Mirror therapy isn't just for paralyzed limbs.
●
●
●
Confidence shattered? Mirror your strong self — act "as if" until it becomes real.
Skill rusty? Watch someone great (or your past self on video) and mimic.
Fear blocking you? Visualize success in the mirror — trick your brain into believing
it's possible.
Reflection Point
What part of your life feels "paralyzed"? Set up a mirror (literal or mental). Watch your
strong side succeed. Imagine the weak side following. Do it daily for 10 minutes. Your
brain can be tricked into healing. Trick it.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Mirror therapy fools the brain — but the results are real. Watch the strong side win. Make
the weak side jealous. Then make it join the party. The Sasquatch didn't wait for
movement — I created it. So can you.
"
Builder’s Note
A mirror, some imagination, and relentless reps — that's all it takes to start rewiring. Don't
wait for the brain to catch up. Trick it into catching up. Keep swinging.Chapter 15: Conquering Drop Foot and
Reclaiming Your Stride / Drop Foot
DESTRUCTION — Walking Like a
Warrior Again
Drop foot was my daily enemy for years. Toes dragged, ankle flopped, every step risked a
fall. Doctors said "permanent nerve damage.
" I said "temporary bullshit.
" Here's how I
destroyed drop foot and reclaimed a natural stride.
Understanding Drop Foot
Peroneal nerve damage → weak anterior tibialis → foot drops → drag toes → trip → fear →
less walking → weaker muscles → worse drop foot. Vicious cycle.
I broke it with a multi-attack strategy.
My Drop Foot Destruction Arsenal
1.
Ankle Dorsiflexion Drills (100+ reps/day)
○
Seated: Lift toes toward shin with band resistance.○
Standing: Heel walks (walk on heels only).
2.
Negative Pressure / Eccentric Loading
○
Stand on step edge. Lower heels below step slowly (eccentric). Rise onto
toes (concentric).
○
Wall leans: Lean forward, press ankles into dorsiflexion under body weight.
3.
Mirror Therapy + Mental Rehearsal
○
Watch good foot lift in mirror. Imagine left doing same. 10–20 min/day.
4.
AFO Ditching Protocol (Risky — Do Safely)
○
Started short walks without AFO (held wall/Michael).
○
Built confidence in ankle stability.
○
Gradual outdoor use. Never fell hard — adjusted when needed.
5. 66-Day Consistency Streak
○
Locked in daily reps for 66 days. Habit formed. Brain rewired. Foot lift
became automatic.
Results
●
●
●
Year 1–2: AFO required, dragging bad.
Year 3: Short walks without AFO possible.
Year 4+: Drop foot almost gone. Walk with little limp. Natural stride reclaimed.
Universal Lesson
Every "drop foot" in life — bad habit, fear, weakness — can be destroyed the same way:
targeted drills, consistency, mental rehearsal, gradual exposure, refusal to accept
"permanent.
"
Reflection Point
What's your "drop foot"
— the thing dragging you down? Write it down. Pick one daily drill
(physical or mental). Commit to 66 days. Track it. Your heart's still thumpin'
. Your life is
still pumpin'
. Get knocked down five times, stand up six.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Drop foot ain't permanent until you quit trying. Attack it daily. Rewire it. Destroy it. The
Sasquatch didn't drag his foot forever — neither should you.
"
Builder’s Note
Drop foot destruction isn't luck — it's reps, refusal, and system. Reclaim your stride. Walk
like a warrior again. Keep swinging.Chapter 16: Arm and Hand Recovery:

2.

2.

wins. Your heart’s still thumpin’
. Your life is still pumpin’
. Stand up six times.Sasquatch’s Steel
"Plateaus aren’t parking lots — they’re launchpads. Stay the course. Invisible work
becomes visible breakthrough. The Sasquatch didn’t quit during the stall. Neither should
"
you.
Builder’s Note

Hidden Recovery / Plateaus are
LAUNCHING PADS, Not Parking Lots
Emotional recovery is the silent half of the war. Physical gains can be measured.
Emotional strength is felt — or not felt. Depression, frustration, isolation, self-doubt —
these tried to kill my comeback harder than any paralyzed limb. Mastering them turned
plateaus into launchpads.The Emotional Battlefield
●
●
●
●
Depression: "Nothing matters. Why bother?"
Frustration: "I’ve worked so hard — why no progress?"
Isolation: "No one understands. Everyone left.
"
Self-doubt: "Maybe they were right. Maybe I am done.
"
I fought all four. Won by treating emotions like muscles — train them daily.
Emotional Mastery Arsenal
1.
Purpose Anchor (Daily) Looked at son’s picture. Remembered promise: "Play
catch with both arms.
" Purpose > emotion.
2. Small Victory Counter Logged every flicker, every second longer standing.
Evidence beats emotion.
3.
Tribe Injection TikTok comments: "You inspire me.
" Answered every one. Isolation
died. Tribe grew.
4.
Dark Day Protocol

○
Minimum action: One rep. No zero days.
5.
Humor Weapon Laughed at absurdity: Pooping to Britney, catheter fight, Hoyer
whale moment. Humor disarms despair.
6.
Reframing Drill Plateau thought: "Nothing’s happening.
" → "Invisible consolidation.
Breakthrough coming.
" Depression thought: "I’m a burden.
" → "I’m building
something for my son and thousands of others.
"

PART V: LIVING YOUR RECOVERY —
BEYOND RECOVERY TO EMPIRE
Chapter 21: Home Again: Adapting to
Your New Reality / Home Sweet Home
— Conquering Daily Life
Coming home from the hospital was supposed to be a victory lap. It felt like walking into a
war zone with no weapons. No nurses on call. No therapy schedule. Just me, a
wheelchair, dead weight left side, and a house that suddenly felt like it was built for
giants. Stairs became mountains. Doorways became choke points. Shower became a
potential drowning hazard. Kitchen counters became unreachable. I was a 250-lbArkansas boy turned prisoner in my own home — fully wheelchair-bound or crawling on
all fours like some kind of broken animal.
But home wasn't the end of recovery. It was where the real fight started. Here's how I
turned that house into my personal training ground and conquered daily life one brutal,
humiliating, victorious rep at a time.
The Home Reality Shock
●
Mobility Hell: Slide board transfers to bed or toilet — one slip and I was on the
●
●
●
floor again.
Bathroom Wars: Raised toilet seat (thank God), grab bars in both showers (my
father installed them), shower chair at first. Sponge baths from Lisa. Catheter
crystallized after two months — removal felt like fire. Blood everywhere. I got
front-row seats to the fight and passed out 10 seconds in. Good thing I love looking
at my own blood so much or I might have really passed out! /sarcasm
Kitchen Nightmare: Counters too high. Couldn't reach anything. Pureed food
phase lingered. Appetite came roaring back — gained 80 pounds chillin' and
wheelin'
. Beard hid the gobbler. Belt sighed in relief twice a day: once putting pants
on, once taking them off.
Isolation Peak: Friends vanished like smoke. Family fights over visitation
restrictions. Depression crushed me — laid in bed 24/7, smoked like a chimney,
fantasized about steering into traffic to end it. Felt like a burden ruining everyone's
life.My father stepped up big: installed a wheelchair ramp on the front porch so I could get in
and out without being carried. Put handrails in both showers. Raised toilet seat. Shower
chair. He didn't say much — just built. That quiet action spoke louder than any pep talk.
The Turning Point — Fatherhood Fire
My son was born while I was still wheelchair-bound. Couldn't even stand for the delivery.
Watched from the chair as his little pale body poked up over Lisa's horizon. That moment
hit like a freight train: I was a father. But what kind of father could I be stuck seated? How
could I play catch, teach him to throw a spiral, show him how to be a man — from a chair?
I made a promise right there: "I'll give everything to be the best father possible. I'll play
catch with both arms.
" That why pulled me out of bed when nothing else could. Purpose >
pity. Fatherhood became my ultimate motivation — the fire that turned home from prison
to proving ground.
Home Adaptation Arsenal
1.
2.
3.
4.
5.
Ramp & Transfer Mastery Father's ramp = freedom to leave house. Slide board
practice daily — from embarrassing falls to smooth transfers.
Bathroom Conquest Raised seat + grab bars = dignity. Shower chair → bench →
standing (with handrail). No more sponge baths. Independence won.
Kitchen Takeover Reacher tool at first. One-handed cooking drills. Grip reps built
strength. Ate for recovery — high protein, BDNF boosters (eggs, nuts, fish). Weight
gain? Fuel for muscle rebuild.
Daily Life Reps Brushing teeth, buttoning shirts, opening doors — forced functional
tasks. Every struggle = rep. TikTok filming = accountability + tribe.
Mindset Shift Home wasn't prison — it was gym. Every task a drill. Every
humiliation a story. Every small win proof: "I'm still thumpin'
. Life's still pumpin'
"
.
Karlie the Caretaker — Compassion When I Needed It Most
Early home days, anxiety was crippling. Couldn't even go into a store without panic. Karlie
became my caretaker. She didn't just help — she showed compassion, companionship,
friendship. Swept my floor, sure — but more importantly, she made me feel human again
when I felt like a burden. That human connection mattered more than any exercise.
Universal Application
Home after any setback is chaos:●
●
●
Job loss? Kitchen becomes job-hunt HQ. Reps = applications.
Breakup? Bedroom becomes reflection zone. Reps = healing actions.
Any grind? Turn space into arena. Fatherhood, family, purpose — find your why.
Heart thumpin'? Life pumpin'
.
Reflection Point
What’s your "home hell" right now? Write one daily rep to conquer it (transfer, task,
mindset). Do it. Turn prison into gym. Get knocked down five times, stand up six.
Sasquatch’s Steel
"Home ain't recovery's end — it's the real arena. Turn stairs into reps, showers into
battles, kitchen into fuel. The Sasquatch conquered his house. Conquer yours. Heart
thumpin'? Life pumpin'
. Keep swinging.
"
Builder’s Note
Home adaptation is where survivors break or rebuild. I rebuilt. You will too. Find your why.
Keep swinging.
Chapter 22: Family & Caregivers:
Managing Your Support Team /
Fatherhood Fire — My Son as Ultimate
Motivation
Stroke doesn't hit just you — it hits everyone. Family becomes battlefield and lifeline.
Caregivers become warriors and casualties. I learned that the hard way. Here's how I
managed my support team — and how fatherhood became the ultimate fire that kept me
fighting.The Family Reality
●
●
●
●
Lisa's Warrior Mode: Pregnant through hell. Rarely left my side — only to shower.
Held pee cup, sponge baths, ripped feeding tube when I choked. Found out it was
a boy weeks after stroke. Stayed. Made call to keep me local vs chopper risk.
We’re not romantic now, but team for Nicholas Jr. Thank you, Lisa — for our son,
for being there when everything was falling apart.
Kasey’s Loyalty: Cousin/brother from another mother. Moms sisters — grew up
together: sports, scooters, weights, even shared opioid addiction in early 2000s
mess. Visited hospital. Relief amid family fights. True brother when others
vanished.
My Father’s Quiet Strength: Installed wheelchair ramp on front porch. Handrails in
both showers. Raised toilet seat. Shower chair. Didn’t say much — just built. Action
> words.
Karlie the Caretaker: Early home days, anxiety crippled me — couldn’t enter
stores. Karlie showed compassion, companionship, friendship. Helped with
mobility and strength training exercises. From upper to lower body on affected
side, she was paramount in helping me regain function. Swept floor, but more
importantly, made me feel human when I felt like a burden.
Managing Your Support Team
1.
Honest Communication Tell them your why.
"This is for my son. For us. For
proving them wrong.
" They fight harder when they know the purpose.2. Give Them Roles Lisa: emotional anchor, daily care. Kasey: bro accountability,
visits. Father: builder/fixer. Karlie: companion/human connection. Define roles —
reduces burnout.
3. Celebrate Together Small wins = family beers (Michael style). Big wins = bigger
celebration. They need victories too.
4.
Protect Their Energy Don’t dump every dark day on them. Have a protocol (kit, call
someone else). Give them breaks. Gratitude goes both ways.
5.
Fatherhood Fire — My Ultimate Why Son born while I was wheelchair-bound.
Couldn't stand for delivery. Saw his pale body poke up. Promise: "Best father
possible. Play catch with both arms.
" That why pulled me from bed when
depression said stay down. Held him first time with both arms? Bigger
breakthrough than any walk.
Universal Application

●
●
●

Addiction? Sponsors as Lisa.
Loss? Family as father.
Any grind? Find your "son"
— your why. Heart thumpin'? Life pumpin'
.

two choices:
"You'll probably never walk normally again,

Class is in session.PART I: THE STORM — THE
BREAKDOWN TO BREAKTHROUGH
Chapter 1: The Day Everything Changes
/ December 3, 2018 — The Day I
Refused to Die
One moment I was built like a brick wall — benching over four hundred pounds, working
long days, feeling unbreakable. Then, the next, half my body went silent. The lights didn’t
go out completely that day; they just flickered. My left side stopped listening. My arm
hung heavy, my leg dead weight, words crumbling on my tongue.
I tried to tell myself it was exhaustion, dehydration — anything but what it was. But the
truth hit like a sledgehammer: I’d had a massive hemorrhagic stroke.
The Body That Betrayed Me
I’d always been the strong one — the athlete, the protector, the worker. Football,
basketball, baseball, gymnastics as a kid. If it took grit, I was in.
So when my body quit, it wasn’t just medical. It was an identity collapse. How does a man
built on strength function when strength is gone? Lying in that hospital bed, I wasn’t
thinking about recovery or inspiration. I was thinking about survival — and the terrifying
thought that my own body had become a prison.
The Chainsaw in My Skull
The pain wasn’t what broke me — it was the confusion. It felt like chainsaws buzzed
behind my eyes. I even reached up once, sure I’d find steel under my scalp. Every beep of
a monitor mocked me. I couldn’t fix this with muscle or willpower. The same body that
once obeyed every command now ignored me completely.
And then came the humiliation. In the ICU, I lost 50 pounds — down to tube feedings, then
pureed foods that tasted like cardboard mush. Doctors said the stroke hit areas that would
wipe out music recognition — I’d never know a Britney Spears song from a chainsaw
again, couldn’t even tell a hot dog from a burger by sight or sound. Boy were they wrong!One day, while dealing with the straight-up embarrassment of pooping myself
(incontinence hitting hard from the brain damage),
"Oops... I Did It Again" blasted in my
head. Not just blasting — I sang it out loud, as loud as I could, which was a damn feat
considering I had just graduated from tubes to purees. Nurses probably thought I was
losing it, but it was proof: music was still there, appetite intact, brain still firing in ways
they said it wouldn’t.
When I got home, it flipped — gained 80 pounds chillin' and wheelin' in my wheelchair. I
grew a beard to hide the gobbler that developed. My belt sighs in relief twice a day: once
when I put my pants on, and again when they come off at night! The weight scale wishes
the docs had locked that one down as fact.
That’s when I realized: recovery wasn’t going to be about strength. It was going to be
about communication — and hope.
Builder’s Science: The First Signal
After a stroke, the brain’s communication network misfires — neurons lose connection to
the body’s "blueprints.
" But under the right conditions, neuroplasticity kicks in: new
pathways form, rerouting messages through surviving tissue. It’s like rewiring a circuit
board after an explosion — slow, delicate, but possible.
Every attempt to move, even a failed one, sends sparks through that rewiring process.
Every flicker counts.
(Reference: Kleim & Jones, 2008; NIH Neurorehab Review, 2021)
The Moment Everything Changed
When I met Michael, my physical therapist, he didn’t look like much — tall, lanky,
unassuming. But he carried belief like a toolbelt.
He didn’t baby me. He didn’t promise miracles. He just said,
Nick. We’re here to rebuild.
"
"We’re not here to survive,
That was the first flicker — not in my muscles, but in my mindset. The idea that maybe,
somehow, I could build again.
From Athlete to PatientMy new sport was standing. My new gym, a hallway lined with parallel bars. Each step
was a victory; each failure was data.
Michael would say,
repetition. Repetition meant growth.
"Don’t think of it as exercise — think of it as practice.
" Practice meant
He had me chase balloons to retrain balance, climb stairs with assistance, and use tools
no textbook mentioned. Every creative challenge sent a new signal to my brain. It wasn’t
about muscle — it was about rewiring.
That’s the thing about recovery: you don’t rebuild by routine. You rebuild by
problem-solving under pressure — one flicker at a time.
The First Flicker
The day my left hand twitched, it didn’t look like much. But to me, it was a miracle. A
spark of life inside a body I’d already grieved. I stared at that twitch like a mechanic
watching a dead engine fire once, just once, after months of silence.
That was the first time I understood what progress really was. Not leaps. Flickers. Not
miracles. Messages.
That tiny movement proved that the lights might dim — but they never truly go out.

to Fight Back / The Two Paths:
Limitation or Liberation
The ICU wasn't just a room — it was hell with beeps and fluorescent lights. It was a
month-long stay that nearly broke me. Morphine turned my brain into a funhouse mirror
— hallucinations where nurses were plotting against me, people who weren't there,
feeling trapped in some medical experiment. Medical explanation: brain swelling and
meds. Real explanation: your own mind turning against you when you need it most.
The Communication & Swallowing Nightmare
Words wouldn't come. My thoughts clear in my head, but my mouth refused to cooperate.
Swallowing? Gone. I started on tube feedings — that damn tube down my throat. One
night I woke up choking on it. Lisa freaked out, yelling for help. Response was slow. She
didn't wait — ripped that tube out like a boss. Blood, chaos, nurses rushing in. We might
not have a romantic relationship anymore, but we're still a team raising our son. That
moment? Pure warrior mode from her.
After tubes came pureed food — tasted like baby mush, took an hour to swallow crushed
meds in applesauce. Doctors said the stroke location meant I'd lose music recognition,and couldn't tell a hot dog from a burger. Wrong again. Britney blasted through the pain,
and the appetite survived.
The Dependency & Humiliation Hits
Everything taken for granted became impossible: rolling over, reaching water, adjusting
the blanket, scratching an itch, bathroom dignity. The psychological hit was brutal —
suddenly dependent on others for basic human stuff. This destroys self-worth. But it also
teaches you to fight for independence like hell.
One day, coming back from rehab, I had to use a slide board to transfer from the
wheelchair to the bed. Mobility was trash. I slipped and fell hard on the tile floor. Couldn't
move. Three people tried lifting me — no chance. They finally called in the Hoyer lift —
hoisting me up like a 20k-pound I-beam on a skyscraper. Degrading. Embarrassing.
Nothing I could do.
Then the catheter story. Two months with that thing in — supposed to be changed and
cleaned regularly. Nope. Crystallized to my member. When discharge came, the nurse
removed it. Hurt like the dickens. Blood dripped down my leg, on the floor. I got front-row
seats to the fight — knocked out cold 10 seconds in.
Lisa's Loyalty During the Chaos
Lisa was pregnant through all this. She rarely left my side — only to shower. Weeks after
the stroke, she found out it was a boy. Our baby boy. She stayed. Held my pee cup, gave
sponge baths, fought the system. Thank you, Lisa — for our son, for being there when
everything was falling apart.
The Darkest Thoughts
Brutal honesty: I wondered if everyone would be better off if I didn't make it. Burden. No
independence. The best days behind me. Depression after stroke is real — it can kill
recovery before it starts. Those thoughts are normal. Dangerous. You've gotta fight them
with purpose.
The Hardest, Most Unbearable, Slow, Yet Uber Rewarding Experience
This stroke has been the hardest, most unbearable, slow grind of my life. Yet it's also the
most rewarding. Forced me to rebuild everything — body, mind, purpose. Taught me hope
is contagious. Purpose is non-negotiable. Without it, I'd have dug my own grave years
ago.The Two Paths: Limitation or Liberation
The ICU is where you face the ultimate choice: Limitation or Liberation.
●
●
Limitation is accepting the doctor's statistics, feeling guilt, and letting dependency
destroy your fight.
Liberation is using the humiliation and the darkest thoughts as fuel, fighting for
every small sign of movement (every flicker), and choosing to rebuild for a purpose
bigger than your pain.
The gap between impossible and inevitable is your refusal to quit. Your hell is your
launching pad.

"Chapter 3: The Compound Effect of

Rock bottom ain't the end — it's the cleanest slate you'll ever get. When everything's

" family

The Choice That Defines Everything
Every morning in recovery, you wake up and make a choice that decides your whole
future. Most people don't even realize they're making it — it happens on autopilot. But
once you see it clearly, you take control.
The choice is simple:
Probable or Possible?
Probable is what statistics and doctors sell you:●
●
●
●
●
Most recover 60–70% at best.
Big gains stop after 6–12 months.
Significant improvement unlikely after 2 years.
Adjust expectations to avoid disappointment.
Be "realistic"
— manage limitations, accept the new normal.
Possible is what happens when you refuse the sales pitch:
●
●
●
●
What if I push beyond the timeline?
What if I experiment until something clicks?
What if I treat "never" as a dare?
What if my brain rewires farther than anyone predicts?
Probable feels safe — predictable, comfortable, backed by experts. But it costs you
everything: your true potential, breakthrough opportunities, the chance to become proof
that the rules were incomplete.
I made my choice in that hospital bed. A doctor looked at my scans and said,
"You'll
probably never walk normally again. We need to focus on helping you adapt.
" In that
instant, I could have nodded, accepted it, learned to live small.
Instead, I treated it as one person's best guess based on limited data. I chose possible.
"Watch me.
"

●

●
●

●

"Your brain can't change.

"You should stop trying.

Research backs this up hard:
●
●

Hope and optimism improve neuroplasticity (belief drives BDNF release,

Belief in continued improvement leads to continued improvement (self-fulfilling

Your mind isn't along for the ride — it's driving the bus. Choose possible every day, and
the bus goes farther.
My Daily Choice Ritual
Every morning I did the same thing:
Instead of "What can't I do today?" → "What might be possible today?"

Instead of "This is as good as it gets" → "This is where the real work begins.

ICU Survivor to Rehab Warrior
You survived the initial blast. Now, the real construction begins. Leaving the ICU is like
getting airlifted out of a war zone—there is relief, but a new, completely different fight
awaits. This isn't about staying alive anymore; it’s about re-learning how to live.
My first stop: Encompass Health, the first of eight inpatient rehab stays. That statistic
alone tells you everything you need to know about the long-haul commitment required.
The ICU was about flickers of life; rehab is about the unglamorous, repetitive, day-in,
day-out grind. This is where you trade the life support machine for a hard hat and a tool
belt.
The Shock of Repetition
In the ICU, every tiny movement was a miracle. In rehab, every tiny movement is a
mandatory repetition. The environment changed from an emergency room to a
construction site.
●
●
ICU Focus (Survival Phase): Stabilize, survive, and keep the lights on.
Rehab Focus (Construction Phase): Repetition, practice, and rebuilding the body’s
blueprint from scratch.It was here I learned the difference between healing (passive, time-based) and
construction (active, effort-based). Healing happens to you. Construction you do yourself.
The first few weeks were a psychological shock: endless transfers, balance drills, and the
soul-crushing weight of knowing this wasn’t a sprint. It was a marathon where every mile
was uphill. The biggest trap in rehab is letting the routine become a rut.
The Law of Forced Growth
The most painful tool in the rehab arsenal was the parallel bars. I stood there, channeling
all my focus into making my left leg step forward. It was a war of nerves. My brain sent
the signal—Move!—but the leg remained dead weight, dragging and scraping the floor.
One session, trying a drill Michael called the "Step-Tap-Hold,
" I stumbled, caught myself
on the bars, and swore out loud. Michael, who always knew the score, just said: "Your
brain is not broken, Nick. It’s rerouting. It needs the map. You have to draw that map with
sweat.
"
That’s the Law of Forced Growth: You have to force the brain to fire new pathways
through relentless, specific, and often frustrating repetition. You can't just wait for the
power to come back on; you have to rewire the house. Discomfort is not the enemy;
comfortable is the enemy.
The Hidden Victory: Community
The ICU was isolating. Rehab was the first time I was surrounded by other
warriors—stroke survivors and traumatic brain injury patients who understood the
"chainsaw in the skull" feeling. We shared the dark humor, the tiny wins (like feeding
yourself without spilling), and the absolute certainty that the person next to you was
fighting the same invisible war.
This community became the first external component of the Sasquatch Armor. Hope is
contagious. You see a survivor walking, and a voice in your head screams,
"If they can do
it, watch me.
" Rehab wasn't just therapy; it was the boot camp where I learned to use
frustration as fuel. It’s where I started transforming from a patient into the architect of my
own future.
Builder’s Blueprint: Early Rehab Focus
1.
Ditch the "Patient" Mindset: You're not sick; you're in training. This is a job. Show
up and punch the clock.2.
3.
